#2867. What we learned as Kings' dreadful home streak continues in loss to Thunder

What we learned as Kings' dreadful home streak continues in loss to Thunder originally appeared on NBC Sports Bay Area

SACRAMENTO — It's been 219 days since Kings fans have seen the beam after a home win at Golden 1 Center.

Sacramento on Sunday dropped its eighth straight home game, extending the team's longest home winless streak to begin a season in franchise history with a 125-103 loss to the Oklahoma City Thunder.

The Kings' last victory at G1C came in April 10 last season against the Golden State Warriors.

The Kings are in danger of dropping all seven games of their season-long homestead if they don't beat the Phoenix Suns on Wednesday. They fell to 1-12 on the year, and remain on pace for one of the worst records in NBA history.

But they still have company at the bottom of the standings. The Utah Jazz and Milwaukee Bucks each fell to 1-13 with losses on Sunday.

The Thunder, who came in with a three-game losing streak, were playing without four key rotation players: Chet Holmgren, Cason Wallace, Jalen Williams and Jaylin Williams.

Which meant the stage was set for OKC's two-time NBA MVP to attack Sacramento's 28th-ranked defense.

Gilgeous-Alexander had 21 points in the first half and 16 in the third quarter. The Thunder went into the fourth quarter with a 14-point lead they extended to 27, and Gilgeous-Alexander didn't have to re-enter the game.

He finished with 37 points on 13-of-20 shooting – included 4 of 5 from 3-point range – and got wherever he wanted on the court. Rookie center Aday Mara finished second on OKC in scoring with 14 points and 13 rebounds in 26 minutes.

Mara had more points and rebounds than Domantas Sabonis, who finished with 11 points, 12 rebounds and three assists in 29 minutes.

Kings prized rookie Darius Acuff Jr. came into the game leading all rookies in scoring, assists and minutes. The 19-year-old has been the focus of opponents' scouting reports and game plans throughout his young NBA career.

It shouldn't be entirely surprising Acuff had one of his most challenging performances against the Thunder, who came into the game with the NBA's fifth-ranked defense.

Acuff had a career-worst nine turnovers. He reached his seventh early in the fourth quarter while no other Kings player had more than one. He led the Kings in scoring with 26 points but also had the worst plus-minus at minus-24 in just under 33 minutes.

Acuff had another volume shooting night, going 10 of 25 from the floor. He's had at least 19 shot attempts in every game this season.

The Kings have made it clear that Acuff is their centerpiece going forward and will take the lumps typical for rookies. But the team will continue to harp on avoiding turnovers.

The Kings were without three players who had started at least nine games in Zach LaVine (illness), DeAndre Hunter (hip soreness) and Precious Achiuwa (left ankle soreness).

Doug Christie added Malik Monk, Dylan Cardwell and Keegan Murray in their places joining Darius Acuff Jr. and Domantas Sabonis.

It didn't get off to a good start. The Thunder made 5 of their first 7 shots and took a 13-4 lead at the first timeout three-plus minutes in before the first substitution.

Christie made a change to start the second half, going with Leaky Black at forward instead with Cardwell on the bench serving as Sabonis' backup. Christie didn't play two bigs together again until garbage time late in the fourth quarter.

Murray, who has been dealing with left ankle capsulitis, was listed as questionable entering Sunday's game before being announced as available roughly 90 minutes before tipoff.

He struggled to get going, making just 1 of his first 5 shots in 17 minutes, adding just a single rebound and no assists. Murray didn't hit his second shot until 2:00 in the third quarter when he made a 3 from the right wing. He went on to airball a contested corner 3 in the final seconds of the period.

Murray finished with just 6 points and 1 rebound in 21 minutes. He made 2 of 9 shots.

The Federal Open Market Committee approved the following statement for release by a 10 â 2 vote:

The Committee decided to maintain the target range for the federal funds rate at 3-3/4 to 4 percent, in support of the Federal Reserve's dual mandate. The Committee is continuing its policy of maintaining ample reserves in the banking system.

Economic activity is expanding at a strong pace. While uncertainty remains elevated owing, in part, to geopolitical developments, domestic spending has been resilient. Productivity growth is strong, and capital investment is robust. Job gains have kept pace with the workforce, and the unemployment rate has changed little.

Inflation remains elevated. The accumulation of more information will help the Committee determine the policy stance appropriate to ensure a timely return of inflation to the Committee's 2 percent goal. The Committee will deliver price stability.

Voting against the monetary policy action were Lisa D. Cook and Beth M. Hammack, who preferred to raise the target range for the federal funds rate by 1/4 percentage point at this meeting.

#2863. 3 things we learned about the Cowboys from the 49ers game

The Dallas Cowboys lost 46-40 to the San Francisco 49ers on Sunday. Here's what we learned.

Over the last three weeks coming into this game, the Cowboys pass rush has picked up the pace, racking up nine sacks over that time. The trend continued this week with Cowboys exploding for five sacks against a stout 49ers offensive line that's only allowed eight on the season. Four of those sacks came in the first half.

The defense continued to apply pressure on Brock Purdy throughout the game, especially from the defensive interior. Unfortunately, at times Brock Purdy was able to clear the rush and make great plays downfield to Christian McCaffrey, George Kittle and others. Still, the effort from the pass rush was a bright spot for the fourth straight week and should continue to improve as the season continues.

Even with the sacks, however, Dallas was gashed on way too many big plays to be competitive for an entire game.

As well as the defense played in the first half (minus one big broken play), they fell apart in the second. Misdirection plays from an offense known for misdirection plays shocked absolutely nobody watching, yet Dallas bit hard time after time. A big end around touchdown from Deebo Samuel seemed to be the play that broke the Cowboys. After that point, the 49ers never looked back, scoring touchdowns on nearly every possession.

The defense couldn't muster much until halfway through the fourth quarter when they finally forced a 49ers punt before giving up the game sealing touchdown on the following 49ers drive.

The defense continues to prove to be one of the most untrustworthy units in professional football.

Dak Prescott and the offense put up 16 points in the first half behind an efficient rushing attack and a strong passing game. George Pickens grabbed two touchdowns in his return back to the field in the first half in a game that should have gone into the half tied if not for a missed Brandon Aubrey extra point. Pickens would go on to finish the day with three touchdowns in total.

Despite the success, however, Dallas would put up just nine points through the second and third quarters until they broke through with 21 points in the fourth. At one point, the 49ers went on a 28-3 scoring stretch. If the defense is going to disappear, the offense needs to stop the bleeding and somewhat keep pace. Fair or not, it's the position this team found themselves this week and oftentimes this season.

At the end of the day, however, any team that scores 40 points should easily win the game. We can talk about the 49ers defense playing a bit soft with a big lead, but that point total should always result in a win. For the 2026 Cowboys, however, losing with massive offensive numbers is extremely unsurprising.

#2859. Junk bonds are 'flashing yellow.' Watch these warning signs

Cracks are forming in the junk bond market as investors demand higher payouts for owning the market's riskiest debt. It isn't time to ditch high-yield bonds, but investors should pay attention to the warning signs.

High-yield bonds now yield 8.1%, up from 7.22% a month ago. The increase reflects a jump in yields across the curve as investors bake in more inflation from high energy prices and other pressures, including concern about the deficit — hitting nearly $2 trillion in the fiscal year that ended Sept. 30.

The high-yield market is also showing stress on the credit side with spreads recently widening to levels not seen since April, according to the Federal Reserve Bank of St. Louis. Credit spreads are the difference in yield between the bonds and Treasurys of similar maturities. Wider spreads mean investors are demanding higher yields for holding corporate debt, viewing it as riskier.

Spreads are at 315 basis points in the overall high-yield market, higher than a year ago but still below levels in March when they reached 346 bps. One basis point equals one one-hundredth of a percent, or 0.01%.

The high-yield market consists of bonds rated BB+ by S&P and Fitch and Ba1 and under by Moody's. The lowest-rated cohort, CCC and below, has seen the most movement with spreads climbing dramatically over the past year to roughly 1,250 bps.

Right now, the high-yield market is "flashing yellow" but is "far from red," said Michael Arone, chief investment strategist at State Street Investment Management.

It makes sense that investors are demanding more compensation for taking on additional credit risk as borrowing costs rise, he said.

Yields are elevated across the board with the 10-year Treasury reaching its highest level since 2002 earlier in the week.

"The bigger question is whether this is simply a repricing of interest rate risk, or the beginning of a more fundamental reassessment of credit quality," Arone said.

He's in a wait-and-see mode since earnings are still growing, interest-coverage ratios remain good, and while default rates have ticked up some, he believes it is not concerning.

That said, the starting point in spreads is likely weighing on investors' psyche, since they are still low by historical standards.

"There's a small margin of error here, which I also think raises the anxiety level," Arone explained. "The compensation that investors are receiving for taking on this credit risk isn't overwhelming relative to history, and therefore subtle changes in credit spreads can be concerning."

While there may be concerns about part of the lower-rated market, the overall high-yield market is in good fundamental shape.

In fact, credit quality is at a record high, with BB bonds making up over 60% of the market compared with 38% prior to the global financial crisis, said Kelley Gerrity, a fixed income strategist at Morgan Stanley Investment Management.

"We've had higher-quality companies coming in, and with higher rates now, you also have more discipline from companies that are more indebted ... just because of the higher cost of capital, so that actually is creating a bit of a healthier picture as well," she said.

The lowest tier of high yield has always been more speculative, since the bonds have a higher default risk. Therefore, it's no surprise that they are the ones seeing the most widening in spreads, said Collin Martin, head of fixed income research and strategy at the Schwab Center for Financial Research.

The overall move in high-yield spreads have been "orderly," he said. "Are there cracks forming? I think the cracks are logical cracks with the lowest-rated bonds and it's too early to say that it's spreading to the broad credit market."

In addition, fluctuations within the CCC-rated and lower spreads have been idiosyncratic, Gerrity added. Morgan Stanley recently broke down the spreads in the lowest cohort into two buckets: performing assets and non-performing assets, which are defined as spreads above 1,000 bps.

The non-performing spread to worst is 2,818 bps, while the performing bucket — the biggest piece of the CCC market — is 461 bps, she said.

"It is isolated," she said. "I'm not necessarily sure that that will go away anytime soon, but it doesn't tell the story of overall concern and flashing caution for credit markets right now."

For now, investors should remain selective within higher yield, Gerrity advised.

"We're looking for the best relative value opportunities, and we think that exists within that single-B cohort, and so we're leaning into that," she said.

Investors should be concerned if there is a steep widening in the broader high-yield market.

But there's scant evidence of stress in the BB cohort. Spreads in that group are at 194 bps, up from 179 bps a year ago — although the move hasn't been in a straight line.

"We're going to be focusing our attention on what are considered some stronger businesses," Martin said. "If we start to see the markets demand higher spreads there also, that's what we'll be looking at to see if risks are really rising."

It's also important to understand that the Federal Reserve is raising rates in a strong economic environment, said R.J. Gallo, chief investment officer of global fixed income at Federated Hermes. The central bank is concerned about an inflation problem amid high fossil fuel prices due to the Iran war, while growth has been stronger than expected, he noted.

"With growth being good as the reason that the Fed is hiking, then you wouldn't expect high yield to blow out because growth means revenues stay up, means cash flow and profitability stays up," he explained.

High yield becomes a disaster when the economy is heading into a sharp economic downturn, Gallo said.

"That's when spreads really widen out," he said. "But a recession is not the odds-on bet. Now, talk to me in six months if the Fed keeps hiking over and over, if oil prices stay high for longer, well, then maybe we will start to wonder."

#2854. Can the Raptors break a double digit losing record against the Knicks?

The New York Knicks are heading to Toronto for a two-game stretch against the Raptors, with games on Sunday and Tuesday nights. The Raptors have struggled mightily against the Knicks lately. Not only did Toronto go 0-5 against the defending champions last year, but they have a 13-game losing streak against New York, with no regular season wins against their division rivals since January 2023. And, as odd as it is to say, the Raptors may be hosting the defending champions at the right time.

This year's Knicks are sputtering along at just 6-6. Their losses have all been by single digits, but their play has been characterized by the same malaise that plagued them in the middle of last season. If Toronto is going to break the losing streak, even though Kawhi Leonard won't be suiting up and Scottie Barnes is listed as questionable, there may not be many more opportunities where the Knicks are more vulnerable.

How do the teams stack up? Let's take a look.

What's been surprising about this year's Knicks is that they're by and large the same squad that absolutely blitzed the Eastern Conference in last year's playoffs. The only rotation player from the title-winning squad who has departed is Mitchell Robinson, and the team isn't particularly old. Despite a short offseason (filled with late-night TV appearances, among other things) and some fatigue, the chemistry that brought the Big Apple its first title in over 50 years should have carried over into the new season. It has not.

NEW YORK, NEW YORK – NOVEMBER 11: Karl-Anthony Towns #32 of the New York Knicks reacts against the Cleveland Cavaliers during their game at Madison Square Garden on November 11, 2026 in New York City. NOTE TO USER: User expressly acknowledges and agrees that, by downloading and or using this photograph, User is consenting to the terms and conditions of the Getty Images License Agreement. (Photo by Al Bello/Getty Images) | Getty Images

Blown pick-and-roll coverages have popped up. Opposing shooters have been afforded too much space. The offense has resorted to the sort of hero ball that led to them falling into a 2-1 series deficit to the Atlanta Hawks, with the team standing around waiting for Jalen Brunson to bail them out. Every starter not named Karl-Anthony Towns has been significantly less efficient on offence, and he hasn't gotten the late-game touches he deserves. Last year's team sported a top-five offence. This year, they're just 13th, and somehow playing at an even slower pace (29th in the league) than last year. And, once again, the Mikal Bridges discourse is in full swing, with head coach Mike Brown trusting Landry Shamet over him in key moments.

That being said, these are the reigning champs, masters of improbable comebacks, and they have a recent history of lollygagging for a while against the Raps, before turning it up in the fourth quarter and putting the game away, and their defence still ranks in the top five in the league, despite its flaws.

Thanks to an NBA Cup quarterfinals match, the division foes actually matched up five times in the regular season last year, with New York winning all five, by an average of 19.6 points. The Raptors struggled with injuries (and, uh, covert tanking) last year, but this was a lopsided matchup.

Karl-Anthony Towns vs Jakob Poeltl and Collin Murray-Boyles and also his own teammates

For much of this season, KAT has been the line bright spot in the Knicks' starting lineup. That lineup has had its fair shares of issues over the last few seasons, before flipping a switch last postseason, and many of those old issues have reared their heads again. Towns is shooting a sizzling 54% from the field and 40% from three so far, but New York periodically forgets they employ a seven-footer who's one of the best shooters on the planet. He possesses a unique skillset that allows him to pull Poeltl out of his comfort zone defensively, and the size to pin CMB under the basket, if his team chooses to feed him on the block. And, with the way they've been playing, that's a big "if."

Even more so than Bridges, OG Anunoby, or Josh Hart getting back on track, the key for New York is making sure to take full advantage of KAT's gifts. If the Raptors can make him a non-factor and bother Brunson enough as a scorer (it should be noted he had offseason wrist surgery and hasn't fully been himself), the Raptors have a shot over these next two games to finally end their years-long losing streak against the Knickerbockers.

#2853. Dolphins vs. Bills odds: Opening lines for Week 11 matchup

The Miami Dolphins fell to 1-8 earlier today after coming up just short of beating the Indianapolis Colts, 24-22. Miami's offense played well enough under quarterback Kyle McCord, making his NFL debut after spending his rookie season on the Philadelphia Eagles' practice squad last year, and the defense did its part to keep Miami in the game. The special teams let down the Dolphins – with kicker Zane Gonzalez missing two field goals and an extra point during the game.

The Dolphins' loss was yet another straight-up loss, but they did enough to cover the 3.5-point spread throughout the week, or the 4.5-point mark to which the spread ballooned on Sunday. Miami is being given an even bigger window for next week's game.

Miami visits the Buffalo Bills in Week 11, the first of two meetings between the AFC East rivals in 2026. According to FanDuel Sportsbook, the Dolphins have little chance against the division-leading Bills. The opening line has the Bills favored by 16.5 points.

Buffalo demolished the New York Jets, another AFC East division opponent, on Sunday, 31-12. The win moved the Bills to 8-1 for the year and keeps them in the top spot in the AFC playoff picture, a game ahead of the Kansas City Chiefs and Jacksonville Jaguars. The winner of tonight's Sunday Night Football game between the Pittsburgh Steelers and Cincinnati Bengals will also reach the 7-2 mark, a game behind Buffalo.

The Bills are -1800 on the moneyline for the straight-up victory, while the Dolphins are +980. The point total opened at 47.5.

McCord will start his second game in Week 11, looking to continue to grow in the Dolphins' offense as he replaces Malik Willis, who is on injured reserve with a groin injury. McCord finished the game 17-for-32 for 209 yards with no touchdown passes or interceptions; he did score a rushing touchdown.

#2844. LeBron James moves past Jason Kidd for 3rd on NBA’s career assist list, 76ers top Pacers 131-124

PHILADELPHIA — LeBron James had 11 assists and moved into third on the NBA's career list, and he added 15 points and six rebounds to lead the Philadelphia 76ers past the Indiana Pacers 131-124 on Sunday.

James, who turns 42 next month, dished one to VJ Edgecombe in the fourth quarter for assist 12,092 to pass Jason Kidd on the career list. John Stockton and Chris Paul are 1-2 in NBA history.

Jaylen Brown scored 31 points and Tyrese Maxey added 27 for the Sixers.

Pascal Siakam led the Pacers with 31 points and former Sixer Kelly Oubre Jr. added 16.

James continued an early habit in his first season with the Sixers by picking his spot to take control of a game.

This one came in the third quarter, right after Sixers fans went wild when seldom-used 7-foot-6 center Tacko Fall checked into the game. James immediately went to work picking apart the Pacers and scored eight straight points to give them an 82-73 lead.

Signed ahead of training camp for backcourt depth, Fall almost immediately strained his left hip and missed the preseason. Coach Nick Nurse said ahead of Sunday's game he still thought the fan-favorite Fall could help the Sixers, but that he had "seen him out there, some form," and that "I might just have to take that leap."

Fall helped in that pivotal third-quarter stretch for James. Fall grabbed an offensive rebound that led to a possession where James would sink two from the free-throw line. James then added a near-uncontested driving layup because of a perfect screen from Fall. James added a 20-footer and a dunk during the run.

Fall grabbed two offensive rebounds in 3 minutes. His final stat line with no points in 5 minutes might be easy to overlook — unlike the towering Fall — but he had a major impact in pushing the Sixers to 11-3.

Joel Embiid sat out for the Sixers in the first set of back-to-back games. A two-time NBA scoring champion, Embiid should return to the lineup Monday at Brooklyn. James is another veteran who generally does not play in back-to-back games and seemed poised to take his turn sitting out against the Nets.

James took a hard foul when he drove the lane against Oubre in the first quarter. James clutched his left wrist for a moment but appeared to show no ill effects the rest of the game.

#2840. Top Wall Street analysts prefer these 3 dividend stocks for stable income

Uncertainty in the Middle East, elevated Treasury yields and fears about an AI bubble continue to keep global markets on edge. Given this volatile backdrop, investors seeking stable income can consider enhancing their portfolios by adding dividend-paying stocks.

However, the vast universe of dividend stocks can make selection challenging. Tracking the ratings of top Wall Street analysts can help shortlist attractive dividend stocks, as their recommendations are backed by in-depth research.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Chord Energy is an independent exploration and production company with long-lived assets primarily in the Williston Basin. With a quarterly base dividend of $1.30 per share, CHRD offers a dividend yield of 3.67%.

In a research note on third-quarter expectations, RBC Capital analyst Scott Hanold reiterated a buy rating on CHRD stock with a price target of $175. "We model production near the top of guidance and continue to see strong performance from new wells and base production," said Hanold.

Specifically, the five-star analyst expects Q3 2026 production at 283 thousand barrels of oil equivalent per day, or Mboe/d, in line with the Street's consensus and near the high end of the 278 to 284 Mboe/d guidance. Hanold said his Q3 2026 earnings per share estimate increased to $5.29 due to lower non-cash depreciation, depletion and amortization.

Meanwhile, cash flow per share estimate moved lower to $13.59 to reflect Hanold's update based on the current backdrop and commodity mark-to-market. He expects Chord Energy to report capital spending of $375 million for Q3 2026, slightly above the Street's estimate of $369 million.

Overall, Hanold remains bullish on CHRD, given its best-in-class balance sheet. He expects a free cash flow payout of 80% in the third quarter, with the possibility of an upside. Hanold expects investors to focus on management commentary on 2027 capital plans, Williston M&A opportunities, and long-term performance of 4-mile wells.

Hanold ranks No. 310 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 14.9%. See Chord Energy KPIs on TipRanks.

Williams, an energy infrastructure company that delivers natural gas, is the next dividend pick. The company recently completed the $5.5 billion acquisition of Momentum Midstream, which will expand its natural gas infrastructure in the Haynesville to meet growing Gulf Coast LNG, power and industrial demand. At a quarterly dividend of $0.5250 per share, Williams offers a yield of 2.89%.

In her latest research note, RBC Capital analyst Elvira Scotto reiterated a buy rating on Williams stock with a price target of $87. Ahead of Q3 2026 earnings, the analyst said that she expects WMB to announce strong quarterly performance.

Scotto raised her estimates to reflect insights from the quarterly catch-up call, the completion of the Momentum Midstream acquisition, and mark-to-market quarter-to-date commodity prices. She expects WMB to report Q3 2026 adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization of $2,036 million, which is modestly above the Street's consensus estimate.

Furthermore, Scotto expects Williams' Transmission, Power & Gulf segment to increase quarter-over-quarter, driven by the startup of the Socrates power project and favorable natural gas liquids, or NGL, margins.

Scotto noted that WMB stock has underperformed due to backlash related to data centers and investor anticipation of another Power Innovation project. She expects the company to announce at least one more project before the end of this year, potentially with a new customer and a contract duration of 15 to 20 years, which would be a positive catalyst for the stock.

Scotto ranks No. 175 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. Her ratings have been profitable 67% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.2%. See Williams Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

EOG Resources, a crude oil and natural gas exploration and production company, will hold its third-quarter earnings call on Nov. 6. At a quarterly dividend of $1.02 per share, EOG offers a dividend yield of 2.75%.

Heading into Q3 earnings, Jefferies analyst Lloyd Byrne reiterated a buy rating on EOG Resources stock and increased his price target to $185 from $175. The analyst expects the company's Q3 cash flow per share to exceed the Street's consensus by 13%, driven by better-than-expected production.

Byrne expects crude oil production, excluding the UAE, to reach 551 Mboe/d, which is at the high end of guidance (546-551 Mboe/d) and above the Street's projection of 548 Mboe/d, driven by continued growth in the Utica.

Furthermore, Byrne expects cash operating expenses to come in at $10.27 per barrel of oil equivalent, below the midpoint of guidance of $10.05 to $11.35, helped by lower lease operating expenses. Overall, the analyst expects EOG to deliver free cash flow of $2.65 billion, supporting another quarter of buybacks near the minimum 70% shareholder return threshold and implying about $1.5 billion in share repurchases.

"As a first indication of the '27 plan, we expect EOG will message another year of mid-single-digit (~3.5%) growth on a flat capital budget (~$6.55bn), which is above the Street modeling 1% oil growth at $6.6bn," said Byrne.

Byrne ranks No. 303 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 58% of the time, delivering an average return of 18.5%. See EOG Resources Statistics on TipRanks.

#2839. Federal Reserve Board announces pricing, effective January 1, 2027, for payment services the Federal Reserve Banks provide to banks and credit unions, and changes to streamline and adjust their check services

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 1:30 p.m. EST Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Thursday announced pricing, effective January 1, 2027, for payment services the Federal Reserve Banks provide to banks and credit unions, such as the clearing of checks, automated clearing house (ACH) transactions, instant payments, and wholesale payment and settlement services.

By law, the Federal Reserve must establish fees to recover the costs, including imputed costs, of providing payment services over the long run. The Federal Reserve expects to recover 101.5 percent of actual and imputed expenses in 2027, including the return on equity that would have been earned if a private-sector firm provided the services. Overall, price changes for 2027 will result in an estimated 1.4 percent average price increase for established, mature services.

In addition, the Federal Reserve Banks announced changes to streamline and adjust their check services. As described in the request for information on check services issued last year, the volume of checks processed by the Federal Reserve Banks is declining, but checks remain an important payment instrument for many consumers and businesses. The changes were informed by public feedback and ensure that the Federal Reserve Banks continue to provide check services efficiently. The adjustments include optimizing check processing windows during low-volume periods, increasing fees that banks and credit unions pay to process checks, and other operational updates. These changes do not impact consumers' and businesses' ability to pay with or receive checks and only impact how banks and credit unions process checks with Federal Reserve Banks.

The entire 2027 fee schedule for all of the Federal Reserve's payment services is published on FRBservices.org®. Additional information on the Federal Reserve's payments services, including service usage statistics, is available on the Board's website.

#2838. 4 takeaways from Rams’ first NFC West win in 2026

The Los Angeles Rams' first NFC West victory of the 2026 season delivered several important takeaways that could shape the team's outlook for the remainder of the year. Their Week 10 win over the Arizona Cardinals was more than just another divisional victory; it offered a look at how much depth the Rams have beyond their superstars. From defensive dominance to offensive consistency, the Rams showed areas of strength while providing a few developments worth monitoring moving forward. Here are four takeaways from Sunday afternoon's road win…

Beaux Limmer. Bill Murray. Xavier Smith. CJ Daniels. Konata Mumpfield. Davis Allen. Yup, a group of Day 3 picks and undrafted free agents. Smith, Daniels, and Mumpfield combined for 143 yards and one touchdown. That doesn't include Tutu Atwell who was not on the team during training camp until after the final round of cuts. He accounted for 1 reception for 31 yards. The four receivers each had an explosive play for the offense.

Beaux Limmer started the game at left guard and found his way under center when Coleman Shelton (hamstring) and Dylan McMahon (knee) were knocked out of the game. Bill Murray then filled the left guard vacancy.

I predicted the Rams defensive front would get going. The defense notched four sacks in the first half. With Aaron Donald recording his first sack since 2023.

This doesn't even include Kobie Turner who should return within the next 2-3 weeks at most.

When Puka Nacua got hurt in 2025, the Rams pivoted to 13 personnel. The Rams have led the trend in the NFL for 13 personnel usage since that moment.

Now with a slew of injuries to Puka Nacua, Davante Adams, and Terrance Ferguson, Sean McVay pivoted his offense again using some 20 personnel. With the strong tandem of

Entering Week 10, Sean McVay did not have a great record against his former assistants. He was 5-9 with 6 losses to the LaFleur brothers. Matt LaFleur is 5-0 against Sean McVay while Mike LaFleur picked up a win over his former boss in Week 6.

Not only did the Rams get a much needed win but this one should be a boost of confidence for McVay going into Week 12 against Matt LaFleur's Green Bay Packers.

#2835. I owe Sam Hauser an apology

I've consistently been a champion of Sam Hauser's game since he first joined the Celtics. But I wavered. As last season wrapped up, I believed his time in Boston was probably winding down.

That wasn't about a lack of belief in his abilities, but I felt the team needed to shake things up a bit, and his contract would give them the easiest pathway to accomplish that. The other reasoning was that they could distribute his minutes to some of their younger wings to fully understand what they've got there.

The problem with that analysis is that it undervalues a long sample of Hauser being flat-out awesome. To start this year he's been as good as ever.

After a "down year" shooting 39.3% from three, he's currently hitting a career-high 43.5%. He's fourth in the NBA in three-point attempt rate, reestablishing himself as one of the league's top marksmen.

With a very fluid offensive approach for Boston this year, Hauser has played a big part in their on and off-ball screening game. He's been the recipient, flying around stagger screens and knocking shots down on the move. He's also brought his usual ghost screen and pick-and-pop ability, creating advantages for himself or the ballhandler.

Paul George has been an extremely natural fit next to him. The two of them have such a ridiculous amount of combined shooting ability that it's hard for an action between them to not throw the defense off.

They each demand so much attention on the perimeter. A brief mix-up here means the defense has to sprint in rotation to Hauser. Then they've left an equally deadly shooter in Payton Pritchard wide open.

Hauser is a key contributor in helping Boston execute their offensive principles. Factors like maintaining proper spacing and generating 2-on-1s are some of his skills that might fly under the radar, but help the Celtics play how they want to.

He's an incredibly threatening off-ball option and has been a staple of Tatum-led bench units that historically crush teams. That's been the case once again this year. With Tatum and Hauser on the floor, the Celtics have a +26.6 Net Rating. I try to avoid treating small samples like gospel, but for those two it's supported by years of data.

Hauser's spacing gives Tatum more room to operate, and Tatum's gravity gives Hauser clean looks. It's a perfect partnership. When you think about building a great lineup around Tatum, Sam is the exact type of fit you need.

It's why I must say that I regret discussing him as a trade candidate. Knowing that Tatum would be back for a full season was enough to make me feel bullish on several players, but Hauser should've been thought of as the primary beneficiary of that. He's always provided a boost to winning basketball games, and he's playing alongside his most compatible teammate.

For this season, Boston has a +20.7 Net Rating swing with Hauser in the game vs on the bench. That number is absurd and will stabilize eventually. It's clear, though, that he is a key to their success in the early part of this season.

Aside from the shooting, he's been good enough on the defensive end to earn some high praise from Joe Mazzulla.

"He's one of the better defenders that we have on our team, because he does a great job of taking away the #1 thing…so detailed in taking away player tendencies," Mazzulla said.

He's a player that can be on the floor in any scenario. Boston is developing their other wings to fit in that mold, but for Hauser there's not a high-leverage moment where the team needs to sub him out.

This was reinforced most recently in the Celtics grind of a win against Toronto.

With the Celtics up three, and the Raptors looking to tie it, it was Hauser that sniffed out the play and came up with a huge steal to seal the game.

The fact that he was in the game for that moment speaks to the level of trust and respect the coaching staff have in his defense. Jordan Walsh and Hugo Gonzalez have the label as stronger defenders, but it's Hauser that's on the floor to close it out. That's not a knock on either of those young wings, but a testament to the level of preparation Mazzulla mentioned.

For Hauser, his knowledge of the system is nearly as important as his shooting ability. This is his 6th season with the team, making him the 3rd longest-tenured Celtic. He's reached a point where he's leaned on for his perspective.

"Overall, I have seen a lot, experienced a lot," Hauser said. "Just trying to help younger guys… and then this year, coaches have wanted me to be a little more vocal and share my thoughts, what I'm seeing."

On a roster that had a serious shakeup, Hauser is a steadying force. You always know what you're going to get from him. He doesn't step outside of his role, and he executes it masterfully. While he may have a tradeable salary, it's the value he provides on a great contract that makes him even more worthy of sticking around.

The Celtics are a better and more cerebral team for having him.

#2834. Agencies extend comment period for proposed third-party risk management guidance

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 4:15 p.m. EST Share

The Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation, the Federal Reserve Board, the National Credit Union Administration, and the Office of the Comptroller of the Currency today announced that they will extend, until December 16, the comment period for proposed guidance on managing risks associated with third-party relationships. Comments on the proposal were originally due November 16.

The proposed guidance reflects the agencies' supervisory experience and lessons learned from examining financial institutions' third-party risk management practices. It is intended to help banks and credit unions tailor their risk management practices to the risks associated with individual third-party relationships. The proposed guidance takes a principles-based approach and, like all supervisory guidance, is non-binding.

When finalized, the federal bank regulatory agencies plan to rescind existing third-party risk management guidance and replace it with the finalized guidance to promote consistency and prudent innovation in the banking industry. NCUA also plans to rescind its existing third-party risk management guidance and will conduct its own reviews to update related guidance as needed.

Also today, the Federal Reserve Board separately announced that it will extend, until December 16, the comment period on a proposed third-party risk management guide specifically for Federal Reserve-supervised traditional community banks.

Federal Register notice: Proposed Third-Party Risk Management Guidance; Extension of Comment Period (PDF)

Federal Register notice: Proposed Third-Party Risk Management Guide for Traditional Community Banking Organizations (PDF)

Agencies seek comment on proposed third-party risk management guidance and issue statement on community bank engagement with core service providers

Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System

#2829. Rising rents draw Wall Street money to industrial outdoor storage lot investments across the U.S.

Nondescript lots holding idle bulldozers, trucks and other industrial equipment are a common sight in local real estate markets across the U.S., and increasingly, a focus for Wall Street private investment giants.

If you haven't noticed them, it's probably because they so easily fade into the landscape, just another non-branded bit of land in the miasma of suburbia. But Brookfield and Blackstone, two of the world's largest alternative asset managers, are among the institutional investors moving in on what the industry calls industrial outdoor storage, or IOS. Investors are eyeing these lots as the AI build-out increases the demand for heavy equipment.

"We have strong conviction in industrial outdoor storage, which plays a critical role in our supply chain," said Devin Barnwell, a managing partner and co-leader of Brookfield's logistics business.

He says that up until now, the segment has been fragmented, but Brookfield sees that changing.

"We see significant opportunity to bring institutional capital and operating expertise to this fragmented sector, improving the quality and efficiency of these assets while strengthening the resilience of our industrial supply chains," Barnwell said.

Brookfield spent $1.2 billion earlier this year to take Peakstone Realty Trust private, a deal completed in May that gave it a portfolio of more than 70 industrial properties, most of them outdoor storage yards.

Blackstone is also ramping up in the sector, mainly as a lender. Its real estate debt arm has made six loans to outdoor storage owners totaling more than $1.1 billion, including a $244 million loan in June to Alterra IOS to buy 37 properties.

Despite the billions being bet on the market by Wall Street, there is still opportunity in the market for individual investors and local entrepreneurs looking to park their money where others park their equipment.

"1,000 percent yes," said Adam Petrillo, an executive managing director at Newmark, a commercial real estate advisory firm, who co-leads its industrial outdoor storage practice.

Petrillo said smaller, non-institutional investors, still control a significant amount of well-placed storage in secondary markets. "The good news for smaller investors and mom and pops is that a good individual IOS site isn't necessarily 50 acres," Petrillo said.

Lots as small as three to five acres in primary or secondary markets can still yield a robust return, he said.  "At that bite size, an individual can step in. We are not talking about tens of millions ... a three-acre, well-located IOS with multiple use cases can be found, marketed, leased,  and stabilized," he added.

Rents for industrial outdoor storage roughly doubled across eight major markets between 2020 and the third quarter of this year, according to data from Newmark Research and Alterra IOS, an owner of such properties. Average rent per usable acre rose 103.5%, to nearly $11,000 a month, compared with a 66% increase for traditional industrial space, such as indoor warehouses.

Rent growth, according to Alterra, is most closely tied to infrastructure and energy activity in the area, even more so than data center development specifically.

The value of these properties can also increase if located near a seaport, rail line, or highway. But the gains weren't uniform. Baltimore (204%), Houston (159%) and Norfolk (148%) led, while South Florida and Savannah IOS actually trailed traditional industrial.

Lisa DeNight, managing director, head of North American industrial research at Newmark, said that Savannah's IOS vacancy remains quite tight despite the rent figures. Recent port activity is a potential factor, according to Alterra. Savannah's container volumes more than doubled between 2010 and 2022 but have since declined, while Houston saw roughly 15% growth from 2022 to 2025.

Petrillo said that there is still plenty of room left for values to increase, so an investor jumping in now hasn't missed the game. "We are still in the very early innings," Petrillo said.

Sam Cheema co-founder of CanTex Capital, a Dallas-based firm that acquires overlooked industrial real estate in Texas, is seeing increased demand for these lots.

"It comes down to supply and demand," he said. "If you need a warehouse you can usually find one. Properly zoned infill IOS sites are much harder to find and every year more of them are converted to warehouses and other uses," he said, referencing multifamily housing units. The scarcity makes tenants sticky. "So a limited supply keeps shrinking. And because so much of the value is in the land itself, owners keep the option to redevelop the site as the area grows," Cheema added.

Because of the imbalance of supply and demand, rents and prices are rising across the board, according to Cheema, but no two IOS properties look alike, which has kept the door open for smaller investors.

"Big institutions want exposure to IOS, but ownership is fragmented across mom-and-pop owners and small businesses, so it's hard to deploy large amounts of capital without first aggregating these assets into portfolios," Cheema said.

Unlike traditional warehouses, there's no standard spec like square footage or clear height to compare them. "That lack of standardization along with the ongoing growth of the asset class, means there's still runway for small investors to find opportunities," Cheema said.

"Financing has improved as more lenders have a better understanding of IOS, but it's still largely local and regional banks," Cheema added.

"If you don't understand the city's plans for the area, you may end up owning a use they're trying to phase out," Cheema said.

CanTex Capital works with the city and neighborhoods before buying to ensure the impact aligns with the community's plan for its future.

Alex Sutterer, an executive vice president at NAI Hiffman, a Chicago-area commercial real estate firm, said that beyond being a place to store big equipment, IOS attracts investors for two main reasons: lower capital expenditure requirements than traditional industrial or multifamily properties and the potential for higher yields.

"Capital improvements are also less specific to individual tenants, so owners can direct spending toward the property's overall functionality," Sutterer said.

For private investors, IOS typically trades at a yield around one percentage point higher than traditional industrial properties, helping attract smaller investors and family offices. "We believe that gap will continue to narrow as more institutional capital enters this space," Sutterer added.

Even for industrial properties with similar yields, he said the credit profile of tenants is generally stronger for IOS.

Shallow-bay, multitenant industrial properties — industrial facilities that tend to be much smaller than big-box distribution warehouses — have comparable yields. But unlike the local businesses that tend to fill up shallow-bay sites, IOS tenants range from local businesses to those with investment-grade credit ratings, offering investors the potential for both yield and creditworthy tenants.

And institutional money is making IOS more competitive.

"Institutional capital has increased competition in IOS, and property values and lease rates have risen overall," Sutterer said.

He added smaller investors can still find opportunities below institutional deal-size thresholds, although that does not mean every smaller deal is priced fairly. "We see opportunities to add value and earn yield in deals below $3 million–$5 million," Sutterer said.

For local investors, that means there is the ability to build a portfolio at a smaller scale and hold the assets for cash flow, or aggregate more than $10 million in assets and seek a portfolio premium when selling to institutional capital.

But buyers need to understand what they are getting into. Due diligence on tenant demand by site and site function, and tenant turnover ratio, are important factors in buying the right asset. Market diversity can also provide protection, Sutterer said.

"In Chicago, no single user category accounts for more than 18% of IOS tenants, helping insulate the market from industry-specific slowdowns," he said. Tenants' tendency to stay and tight vacancy rates nationally are current advantages of the asset class. In Chicagoland, for example, vacancy is below 3%.

Still, all the due diligence in the world can't protect investors from some variables.

"No one outside the Federal Reserve can control the cost of debt," said Kellie McIvor, vice president of acquisitions at Redwood Living, an Ohio real estate development firm. She said that right now, the cost of debt for light industrial uses is lower than, say, for residential construction.

"However, interest rates are still high, with no relief in sight," McIvor said.

The Fed is expected to raise rates in December in its effort to tame inflation, and after this week's latest hot October CPI report, potentially hike twice more in early 2027, according to some Wall Street forecasts.

"Because lenders view them as riskier than a large institutional investor, smaller buyers can face more expensive debt and may be asked to bring more equity to the deal. That can make financing a small acquisition disproportionately expensive. ... More expensive debt means less room for error in execution," McIvor said.

A small buyer looking at a small site that requires no improvement may be able to fund the deal all on his own. "That's the dream! Own your business and the land it's on, outright!" she added.

Ross Blankenship, who owns two fenced contractor and fleet yards in Colorado's Vail Valley through Wins Parking, said the math has to work today. In a mountain market where little land is zoned for the use, he rents spaces for $210 a month for a car or pickup and $265 for a box truck or trailer, and charges $1,250 to $5,500 a month for contractor zones. Blankenship also runs Park Graph, the parking software he uses to rent the spaces.

The swing factor is vacancy, he said. "A full yard throws off more cash per dollar of capex than a small retail or office building. An empty one earns nothing," he said.

His advice for a first-time buyer: call 10 local contractors and ask what they pay for space.

"A small investor can still buy, but the deal has to work on rents you can collect today, not on what an institution might pay you later," Blankenship said.

#2827. It’s time for Basketball: San Antonio Spurs vs Utah Jazz

Welcome to the Game Thread. Veterans of the Game Thread know how we do things around here, but for all you newbies we have a few rules. Our community guidelines apply and basically say be cool, no personal attacks, don't troll and don't swear too much.

The Spurs are looking to extend their win streak to a dozen on an odd single game road trip to Utah for a Sunday afternoon game. The Spurs have already played the Jazz twice this season (three times, if you count preseason), and will likely finish up the season series this afternoon1. The Jazz are 1-12 on the season so far, with their only win being on Game 2 of the season against the Pelicans. Their two best players (Markkannen, Jackson Jr.) are injured, and the best player on the active roster right now might be rookie Darryn Peterson, who can score in bunches but doesn't play of lot of defense. On paper, this looks like an easy win for the Silver and Black, coming off a 37 point wipeout of the Golden State Warriors on Friday night, who were without the services of their superstar player for the contest.

NBA games are played on the court, so a paper advantage only matters if you put the opponent away early. One thing that we also saw in the Bulls game is that the Spurs can get away with only playing one half of good basketball against a bad team and still win going away. There are two games in the season that will be scheduled once the NBA Cup tournament field is set, and there are some unlikely scenarios that involve another meeting between the Jazz and Spurs.

Darryn Peterson sets a record for shot attempts in a game today, but will be nowhere near the record for made baskets.

#2824. Top Wall Street analysts are bullish on the growth potential of these 3 stocks

Fears around the artificial intelligence trade and higher pressure on oil prices are weighing on global markets, but a few stocks offer investors solid growth prospects even in choppy times.

Investors with a long-term horizon can look beyond the ongoing volatility and track the recommendations of top Wall Street analysts to pick attractive stocks with strong long-term growth potential.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Health insurer UnitedHealth delivered better-than-expected results for the third quarter of 2026. The company also raised its full-year earnings outlook, reflecting continued progress on its turnaround plan.

Following the Q3 print, Morgan Stanley analyst Erin Wright reiterated a buy rating on UNH stock with a price target of $529. "UNH's beat & raise reinforces turnaround momentum, with improving MA [Medicare Advantage]/Optum Health supporting a higher '26 EPS baseline & upside to '27 consensus," said Wright.

The five-star analyst added that better-than-expected performance in the UnitedHealthcare and Optum Health businesses supported another upgrade to the company's 2026 adjusted earnings per share outlook to at least $20.50. Wright noted that UNH raised the earnings guidance despite incremental investments planned for the fourth quarter of 2026.

Furthermore, Wright highlighted management's view that the consensus 2027 EPS of $22.63 was too conservative. Reaching even the low end of the company's 13% to 16% long-term EPS growth target would imply an EPS of $23.17.

Wright raised her 2026 EPS estimate to $20.65 from $19.82 and 2027 EPS to $23.24 from $22.54, reflecting the latest upside and an improving 2026 medical loss ratio to 87.4% from 88.1%. Overall, she believes that UNH's operational performance continues to improve, increasing her confidence in its UHC strategy and Optum Health turnaround execution.

Wright ranks No. 264 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. Her ratings have been successful 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 15%. See UnitedHealth Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Social media giant Meta Platforms has been making headlines since the launch of its Muse AI agent, which is seeing solid traction. Investors look forward to updates on the company's AI strategy when it announces its third-quarter results on Oct. 21.

Heading into Q3 earnings, Guggenheim analyst Michael Morris maintained a buy rating on Meta stock and increased his price target to $850 from $700, reflecting higher estimates for 2027. He expects Muse usage momentum and engagement levels to shape investor sentiment into the Q3 earnings call.

Specifically, Morris highlighted that, according to Apptopia, Muse reached 1.3 million daily active users in five weeks versus 300,000 in the first week. Given that Muse's current adoption represents less than 0.05% of Meta's 3.6 billion daily active user base, the analyst expects management to prioritize boosting usage over near-term monetization.

"Over time, we believe Muse monetizes primarily through businesses paying to reach consumers through their agents, consistent with the economics of Meta's core advertising business, rather than consumer wallets," said the five-star analyst. He added that the checkout integrations with Shopify and PayPal reflect this framework.

While there are concerns that a rival could overtake Muse or the recently launched Muse Wonder and push Meta into a prolonged cycle of overinvestment, Morris remains bullish due to the company's strong record in scaling WhatsApp and competing strongly with TikTok via Reels as well as free distribution to an extensive user base.

Morris ranks No. 629 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.2%. See Meta Platforms KPIs on TipRanks.

Next is semiconductor company Marvell Technology. Earlier this month, Marvell held its Investor Day, where it raised its total addressable market estimate to $400 billion by 2030, reflecting solid AI-led demand tailwinds.

After hosting a non-deal roadshow with Marvell's senior management following the Investor Day, Stifel analyst Tore Svanberg reiterated a buy rating on the stock with a price target of $370.

The five-star analyst said that a major focus of the roadshow was the XPU and XPU Attach mix, as Marvell's Custom business expands toward its FY31 revenue target of $30 billion. In this regard, management reiterated that it expects a balanced mix of XPU and XPU Attach revenue in FY31, though it noted that either segment could grow rapidly enough to shift the mix toward 60/40 or 40/60.

While XPU drove most of the nearly $1.5 billion Custom revenue in FY26, Svanberg expects Marvell's XPU Attach business to grow substantially through the end of the decade, leading to a more balanced mix in this business. At the Investor Day event, the company raised its FY29 Custom target from more than $10 billion to over $12 billion, mainly driven by strength in the XPU Attach business.

Svanberg said that another key topic in the roadshow was whether Marvell can secure enough capacity to meet its raised targets. Management said that it met with suppliers ahead of Investor Day to share its five-year goals and received commitments to support these targets. The analyst believes that Marvell's supply chain commitments give it a competitive edge over rivals as customers seek reliable partners for large-scale infrastructure buildouts.

Svanberg ranks No. 91 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 58% of the time, delivering an average return of 31.8%. See Marvell Technology Statistics on TipRanks.

#2823. Sixers Bell Ringer: Jaylen Brown notches season-high against Pacers

Bell Ringer Standings:Tyrese Maxey – 3.5 VJ Edgecombe – 3Jaylen Brown – 2.5Joel Embiid – 1LeBron James – 1Anfernee Simons – 1Dean Wade – 1

No nap, no problem for the Sixers. They defeated the Indiana Pacers 131-124 on Sunday afternoon, moving to 11-3 on the season.

Joel Embiid was out with plantar fasciitis management, and LeBron James returned to action following his absence against the Raptors.

The Sixers' interior defense let the Pacers run up their total in the first half, but the Sixers' offense proved too much for Indiana in the latter half. Three Sixers finished with six or more assists in what was a transition-filled effort.

Jaylen Brown: 31 points, 12-of-18 FG, 3-of-6 3PT, 5-of-6 FT, 2 assists, 2 rebounds, 1 steal

Jaylen Brown came out the gates firing against Indiana, making seven of his first 10 shots and scoring 16 points through the first 14 minutes of game time. Most of his work came in the midrange, creating space from the defender with the fadeaway and draining it time and time again.

He showcased his pose and ball-handling in the post, understanding that the double team likely will not come to help, and using his length and athleticism to get the shot over the defenders.

His focus appeared reengaged after last week's dud against the Celtics and sloppy night against Toronto on Thursday. He nabbed a steal early in the first half, and made three of six attempts from deep.

It was Brown's most efficient scoring game of the season.

Tyrese Maxey: 27 points, 9-of-19 FG, 5-of-10 3PT, 4-of-5 FT, 6 assists, 4 rebounds, 1 steal

Maxey continues to be the ringleader for the squad, intersplicing his speed drive, pullback crossover and step back to keep the defense off balance. His change of pace ability allows him to stay a step ahead of the defense, and his supporting cast lets him take advantage of the space he creates with ease.

Paired with his marksman ability to shoot, the Pacers were effectively unable to guard him. Maxey found success at all three levels on Sunday.

His passing has also been crucial to the Sixers' offensive success, and he displayed it tonight with six assists. He found multiple guys in transition, but none better than this lob to LeBron James on a fastbreak.

LeBron James: 15 points, 5-of-8 FG, 5-of-6 FT, 11 assists, 7 rebounds, 1 block

His passing ability opens up the floor for everyone, and was evident against the Pacers. With his 11 assists, he passed Jason Kidd on the NBA's all-time assists leaders. He added crucial buckets inside and helped push the pace for the Sixers in transition.

James is doing everything his body is allowing him, filling lanes in transition and hitting cutting teammates with slick bounce passes on multiple occasions against Indiana.

#2822. Predators' Win Streak Snapped in San Jose as They Fall 4-2

As a result, Saros was well out of position when the puck landed on Macklin Celebrini's stick in front of the net. Celebrini then put it away to make it a 4-1 game, putting the final nail in the coffin of Nashville's win streak. Steven Stamkos would score a late consolation goal though, cutting the Sharks' lead to 4-2.

#2820. Lakers share update on Austin Reaves’ injury: a ‘pain tolerance’ issue

Lakers coach JJ Redick gave an update on Austin Reaves' left heel bruise before the team played the Suns on Sunday.

"He's improving," Redick said. "It really comes down to just like a pain tolerance thing. Again, nothing structural beyond a bruise. And so I think every day it's been better, and we'll see where he's at in 48 hours when we play on Tuesday."

Austin Reaves #15 of the Los Angeles Lakers shoots a free throw against the Milwaukee Bucks on Wednesday, Nov. 11, 2026, at Fiserv Forum in Milwaukee. NBAE via Getty Images

Reaves suffered the injury in the third quarter of the Lakers' 123-112 win over the Bucks on Wednesday. After subbing himself out of the game, Reaves had X-rays that were clean.

He was expected to get an MRI when the Lakers returned to Los Angeles on Thursday, but the team's medical staff and Reaves jointly determined that he didn't need further imaging after seeing how he felt that morning.

Los Angeles Lakers' Austin Reaves (15) dunks between Jaime Jaquez Jr. and Kyle Kuzma during an NBA basketball game, Thursday, Nov. 12, 2026, in Milwaukee. AP Photo/Aaron Gash

Reaves is averaging 24.7 points (51.3% from the field, 40.9% from beyond the arc), 3.3 rebounds and 4.7 assists per game for the Lakers, who entered Sunday's game tied for the best record in the league (11-2).

Reaves is the 15th-leading scorer in the league this season.

Of the players ahead of him, only Anthony Davis, Jamal Murray, Victor Wembanyama and Giannis Antetokounmpo are shooting as high of a field goal percentage. And only Murray and Steph Curry are ahead of him in 3-point percentage.

Reaves signed a four-year, $180 million contract with the Lakers this summer, the richest contract in NBA history for a player who went undrafted. He has been instrumental to the Lakers' hot start. Entering Sunday's game, the Lakers had won seven straight contests.

Los Angeles Lakers' Austin Reaves drives against Milwaukee Bucks' Nate Ament on Wednesday, Nov. 11, 2026, in Milwaukee. AP Photo/Aaron Gash

Reaves was ruled out Saturday for Sunday's game. He's day-to-day ahead of Tuesday's contest against the Hornets.

Because of a favorable break in the schedule, which included three days without games, Reaves will have six days of rest and he will have only missed one game by Tuesday.

#2819. Cardinals HC Mike LaFleur Shares Team Plans for Next Few Weeks

Things went from bad to worse this season for the Arizona Cardinals on Sunday. They played host to the Los Angeles Rams, and the Rams dropped a hammer to the tune of a 34-6 win.

That had come in what had been an exciting day for Cardinals fans. Rookie Carson Beck made his first start, and there was hope he could prove to be the future of the position for the Cardinals. Except that didn't happen, and he struggled to the tune of 2 interceptions.

For Cardinals head coach Mike LaFleur, it's been a difficult first season in Arizona. Injuries have taken their toll, especially on the offensive side of the ball. Then, he's had to deal with a controversy at the quarterback position all season long. That's made it difficult to get off the ground, and the Cardinals are now just 3-7 with seven games left.

The Cardinals aren't officially out of the playoffs just yet. They could go 7-0 to end the season and finish 10-7 on the season. That might be enough to push for a Wild Card spot in the incredibly competitive NFC.

Of course, nobody expects that to happen, particularly with the injuries that the Cardinals are dealing with. Not even Mike LaFleur seems to think that the Cardinals are playing for wins in any individual game. Instead, he's more focused on evaluating the roster in the coming weeks.

"We've got what we've got. … There is no magic wand, no magic savior," LaFleur said.

Injuries are certainly going to force depth on the Cardinals offense to try to play more than they'd normally expect. It's an opportunity for whoever does step up during that time. Then, if nobody steps up, that will be something that the coaching staff can take as a lesson as well.

It's a difficult reality for the Cardinals, though. 2021 was the last time Arizona had a winning record or made the playoffs. 2015 was the last time they made the playoffs at all. It's been a long time rebuilding, and the Cardinals aren't done rebuilding just yet.

Perhaps no position is more important to evaluate than quarterback. There, Gardner Minshew is injured, Jacoby Brissett has been traded, and rookie Carson Beck is going to get an opportunity over the next few weeks.

It was a slow start to Beck's time as a starting quarterback in the NFL. The 24-year-old rookie was 11 for 18 passing for 108 yards and 2 interceptions. He was sacked 5 times and had just 1 yard rushing.

Arguably, the interceptions weren't Beck's fault. One was in the arms of a receiver, but the ball was dropped and ended up picked off by Kam Kinchens. On the second, Mike LaFleur said that Tyler Allgeier ran the wrong route. So, a big part of Beck's issues go back to the supporting cast around him.

Beck will get more opportunities down the stretch. That starts with a road trip to play the Kansas City Chiefs next week.

#2818. Meet the seven co-founders of Anthropic

Four researchers, a former journalist, a public policymaker, and an engineer founded Anthropic in 2021. Six years later, the artificial intelligence company is about to go public, with trading expected to begin on Nov. 19, in what will be one of the biggest IPOs on record.

Those seven co-founders are poised to each be worth many billions of dollars.

At the end of 2020, siblings Dario and Daniela Amodei were vice presidents at OpenAI, the company that kicked off the generative AI frenzy with the launch of ChatGPT. But the two had grown frustrated with their employer over its approach to AI safety. Fearing a world where AI models' capabilities would progress faster than our understanding of them, the duo launched Anthropic early the next year.

They took a handful of OpenAI colleagues with them: Tom Brown, the company's research engineering lead on GPT-3; Jack Clark, a policy director; Jared Kaplan, a contract research consultant; and Sam McCandlish and Chris Olah, both members of OpenAI's technical staff.

They were all deeply concerned about OpenAI's ambitions to focus on commercialization, and they now stand to be some of the first to cash in on the AI boom.

"They really started Anthropic not because they were hoping to get rich, but they started Anthropic because they like wanted to shape this really important thing in the world, and they thought that other people might do it worse than them," said Jeffrey Ladish, who joined Anthropic as a contract safety engineer in September 2021, and now serves as the executive director of AI safety institute Palisade Research.

Collectively the co-founders own about 10% of Anthropic. Each has about 240,000,000 class A shares, or about a 1.4% stake.

During Anthropic's most recent funding round, those shares — valued at $58.90 each — amounted to about $14 billion in value for each co-founder. If Anthropic's IPO reaches its target of $70 to $78 per share, or a market valuation of nearly $1.4 trillion, it would push those stakes to $17 billion to $18 billion.

"It's extremely hard not to be influenced by your incentives," Ladish said. "You have investors, and money is not just like an end; it's also a means."

Anthropic did not respond to requests for comment.

The face of Anthropic is CEO Dario Amodei. He holds the most shares of the bunch: 245,259,100 including shares subject to voting proxies.

Amodei, 43, didn't get his start in business. He comes from the world of research, obtaining a doctorate in computational neuroscience from Princeton University in 2011. He then went to work at Stanford University as a postdoctoral scholar from 2011 to 2014.

Even in the private sector, Amodei's experience was focused on research. He worked at China's Baidu, leading research on speech recognition from 2014 to 2015, followed by a stint at Google Brain working on deep learning and neural networks, foundational scientific frameworks for today's large language models. He joined OpenAI in July 2016.

On the Big Technology podcast in July 2025, Amodei said he got into AI to solve the "almost intractable" and "slow moving" problems of biology.

"I think my reason to start a company was that I had worked at other companies and I just didn't feel like the way those companies were run was really oriented towards trying to have that impact," he said, adding that the desire for impact was often used by companies as an insincere ploy to recruit talent.

At OpenAI, Amodei is credited with co-inventing reinforcement learning from human feedback (RLHF), alongside Anthropic co-founder, Tom Brown. RLHF is a fine-tuning method used to align model outputs with human needs. That could mean training a model to speak politely or to not offer violent or harmful solutions to problems.

Four years her brother's junior, Daniela Amodei, 39, serves as the president and board chair of Anthropic. The Amodeis are both San Francisco natives, growing up in the Mission District, just a few miles away from Anthropic's current headquarters.

The pair were teenagers during the height of the dot-com bubble and its subsequent fallout, both attending Lowell High School, a magnet school and one of the most prestigious public schools in the country. After graduating, Daniela went on to study English literature and politics at the University of California, Santa Cruz, earning her degree in 2009.

Daniela started her career in politics, working for Matt Cartwright, a Democrat representing what was then Pennsylvania's 17th congressional district, and then moved into tech, taking on various roles in risk management at Stripe for five years.

In a June interview with Stanford's Graduate School of Business, she said her experience in politics, specifically campaigns, naturally led her to startups and Silicon Valley.

"I was like, 'Wow, a small number of people that work really hard, who are young and driven, can really change the world,'" she said.

Anthropic's chief compute officer, Tom Brown, 39, is perhaps the most entrepreneurial of the bunch. After graduating from the Massachusetts Institute of Technology with a Master of Engineering in 2010, Brown went on to found two startups, MoPub and Grouper.

MoPub was a mobile advertising business acquired by Twitter in 2013. There he served as founding engineer in 2011. Grouper, where Brown served as co-founder and technology chief, was a Y Combinator-backed group dating app.

After Grouper, Brown pivoted to research, sharing on Y Combinator's Lightcone podcast in August 2025 that he was self-taught on machine learning research, a daunting task since he received a B- in linear algebra at university.

"I kind of flip-flopped on it for six months trying to build up courage to" make the switch, he said.

Alongside co-inventing RLHF, as a researcher at OpenAI, Brown led the training efforts on GPT-3, a model that marked a historic leap from GPT-2, going from 1.5 billion parameters to 175 billion.

Jack Clark, 37, the head of public benefit at Anthropic, left his career as a journalist in 2016 to work at OpenAI. Clark is now also the leader of the Anthropic Institute, the company's internal think tank that explores AI's social and economic impacts.

A native of the U.K., Clark grew up in the seaside town of Brighton and later studied English literature and creative writing at the University of East Anglia. He then went on to write for publications including ZDNet UK, The Register, and Bloomberg.

In an email to a colleague at Bloomberg at the time, Clark said his decision to leave journalism was because he had "never believed in something strongly before" and that he needed to join OpenAI "and live with the consequences, which I hope are good."

Kaplan, 42, Anthropic's chief science officer, still holds a position as an associate professor in the department of physics and astronomy at Johns Hopkins University. The theoretical physicist got his PhD in physics from Harvard in 2009, working for four years after as a postdoctoral fellow at the SLAC National Accelerator Laboratory, a particle physics research facility run by Stanford and funded by the Department of Energy.

Kaplan co-pioneered scaling laws, or the notion that large language models get exponentially better when trained on larger and larger data sets, alongside Dario Amodei. He also pioneered "constitutional AI," or a method of aligning models by using a list of safety principles and policies. His day-to-day work concerns safety assessments for models, responsible scaling policy at the company, and existential risks.

In an interview with The Guardian in December 2025, Kaplan noted that the decision over whether or not to let AI "recursively self improve," or write the code to enhance itself without a human in the loop, would be "in some ways the ultimate risk, because it's kind of like letting AI kind of go," and that he expects this risk will be taken sometime between 2027 and 2030.

Anthropic's chief architect, Sam McCandlish, 36, came to OpenAI as a fellow focused on AI safety. He also led early research on coding tool Codex, which now constitutes a significant portion of OpenAI's revenue.

McCandlish claims he and the rest of the Anthropic co-founders were not following an entrepreneurial spirit when they charted out on their own. In a December 2024 video from Anthropic's YouTube channel, he said, "None of us wanted to found a company. We just felt like it was our duty."

McCandlish, like Kaplan, also came from the world of academia, completing his PhD in theoretical and mathematical physics at Stanford in 2017 and working at Boston University as a postdoctoral fellow the year afterward. His work focused on quantum gravity and tensor networks.

Now, his work focuses on pre-training, or the first step of training a large language model on swaths of data, among other things.

Chris Olah, 34, heads Anthropic's interpretability research, a branch of study in machine learning that attempts to understand how a large language model "thinks" — or rather, which data inputs and reasoning methods it uses to arrive at its outputs.

Like McCandlish, Olah was not originally keen on starting a company. In the same December 2024 YouTube video, Olah said, "I was arguing for a long time that we should do a nonprofit instead, and just focus on safety research."

Olah had a nontraditional path to Anthropic, dropping out of the University of Toronto after one year and working for short stints as a developer at the government agency Environment Canada and software startup Xelerance.

His career truly took off when he was awarded a Thiel Fellowship in 2012, which he used to pursue research pertaining to 3D printers for two years. He then joined Google Brain, first as an intern and then as a researcher, before moving to OpenAI in 2018.

While together the seven founders may only own about 10% of total equity, they will have a majority vote. Founder LLC, the legal entity set up that owns a single Class F share, gives Anthropic's co-founders a 50.1% majority voting control over the company. While Benjamin Mann, a member of Anthropic's technical staff and also an ex-OpenAI employee, is often called a co-founder, he is not an owner of Founder LLC.

The co-founders are expected to collectively sell over 20 million shares during the IPO, resulting in roughly $250 million in cash per founder, according to Anthropic's prospectus.

They plan to dedicate 80% of the wealth from their personal Anthropic equity to philanthropy, according to the IPO filing, though the pledge isn't legally binding. Anthropic is also setting aside 5% of its shares to form a new public benefit fund — to finance nonprofits, research, and potentially direct community grants — with potential to grow the size of the fund as the company itself grows.
