#2817. Top Wall Street analysts prefer these 3 dividend stocks for stable income

Global stock markets remain volatile amid persistent geopolitical tensions and concerns about the sustainability of AI trade. Given this backdrop, investors seeking steady income can add dividend-paying stocks to their portfolios.

Tracking recommendations from top Wall Street analysts can help investors pick attractive stocks, as these experts assess a company's ability to pay dividends consistently.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Integrated oil and gas giant Chevron (CVX) is a dividend king. The company has increased its dividend for 39 consecutive years. At a quarterly dividend of $1.78 per share (annualized dividend of $7.12 per share), CVX offers a yield of 3.14%. Chevron will announce its third-quarter results on October 30.

In an earnings preview note on U.S. oil majors, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reiterated a buy rating on Chevron with a price target of $241. The analyst is focused on the company's international growth prospects across Latin America, the Middle East, West Africa, and the Eastern Mediterranean.

Specifically, Mehta highlighted management's commentary on solid execution in Venezuela, with gross production expected to more than double to nearly 600 kbd (kilo barrels per day) by 2031 via three joint ventures. He also noted a recent oil and gas condensate discovery in offshore Angola and is awaiting commercial updates on the West Qurna 2 and Nasiriyah projects in Iraq.

Furthermore, Mehta expects CVX to report improved productivity and capital efficiency across domestic shale assets. He expects the improvement mainly in the Permian Basin, where Chevron projects advanced chemical treatments to drive about a 10% recovery uplift in new wells and bring down capital spend per barrel by about 25% year-over-year. Overall, Mehta expects Chevron to report Q3 2026 EPS of $4.95, above the consensus of $4.89.

"While unfavorable timing effects and volatile commodity prices may present quarterly earnings headwinds, we highlight healthy U.S. refining margins and strong asset utilization as partial offsets," said Mehta.

Mehta ranks No. 381 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 12%. See Chevron KPIs on TipRanks.

Kinetik Holdings (KNTK) is a fully integrated, pure-play, midstream company operating in the Delaware Basin. Earlier this month, the company declared a dividend of $0.81 per share, payable on October 30, 2026. With an annualized dividend of $3.24 per share, KNTK offers a yield of 5.82%. Kinetik will announce its Q3 earnings on November 4, with a conference call scheduled the next day.

Ahead of Q3 2026 earnings, Goldman Sachs analyst John Mackay reiterated a buy rating on Kinetik stock and slightly raised his price target to $58 from $57. He expects the company to report Q3 2026 EBITDA of $279 million, higher than the Street's expectation of $270 million but lower than the second quarter's EBITDA of $281 million.

The 5-star analyst expects Kinetik's Q3 performance to gain from higher gas volumes after notable curtailments in the second quarter. Mackay also expects the quarter to benefit from higher production and steady commodity margins, even though some lower-margin contracts and weaker Waha spread marketing gains could weigh on results.

Overall, Mackay increased his Q3 2026 estimates to reflect higher volumes, driven by rapid Permian egress pipeline commissioning, stronger throughput at Kings Landing I, and higher M2M commodity prices, offset by increased costs.

"As natural gas takeaway constraints ease across the Permian, investor focus centers on KNTK's YE26 volume trajectory and durability of its multi-year growth," said Mackay.

Mackay ranks No. 569 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.9%. See Kinetik's Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Moving to Crescent Energy (CRGY), an exploration and production company with activities focused in the Eagle Ford, Permian, and Uinta Basins. With a quarterly dividend of $0.12 per share (annualized dividend of $0.48 per share), CRGY's dividend yield stands at 3.56%. Earlier this month, Crescent announced an agreement to acquire Eagle Ford assets from Devon Energy (DVN).

Heading into Q3 earnings on Nov. 2, Evercore analyst Stephen Richardson reiterated a buy rating on Crescent stock and slightly lowered the price target to $17 from $18, citing dilution from new shares.

"CRGY's recent acquisition of DVN's Eagle Ford assets marks a strategic, growth-oriented move, potentially enhancing cash flow performance," said Richardson.

The 5-star analyst noted that the acquisition of the Eagle Ford assets at a net purchase price of $3.85 billion will add about 68 MBoe/d of net production and over 600 net Tier 1 locations. Richardson added that a solid track record of synergies from past deals in the Eagle Ford region increases confidence about Crescent achieving efficiencies with this deal.

Additionally, Richardson noted that the financial implications of the acquisition seem impressive, with a projected increase in cash flow per share of about 9% for 2027 to 2029.

The analyst believes that Crescent is not just expanding its business through acquisitions but positioning itself as a more durable company, regardless of market conditions.

Richardson ranks No. 478 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.2%. See Crescent Energy Statistics on TipRanks.

#2816. Sharks Win Second Straight, Defeat Predators 4-2

On the second half of a back-to-back against a team on a seven-game winning streak, the odds seemed stacked against the San Jose Sharks on Sunday night. Instead, they were able to pull off one of their best performances in recent memory as they secured a 4-2 victory over the Nashville Predators.

The Sharks had a strong start, keeping the Predators without a shot for nearly 11 minutes while generating quite a few chances of their own. Eventually, the Sharks were rewarded for their efforts as Will Smith picked up a perfectly placed pass from Ivar Stenberg that sprung him on a breakaway. Smith beat Juuse Saros to give the Sharks a 1-0 lead at 12:25 in the first period.

Collin Graf broke his 11-game goalless streak with a strange bounce that made its way past Saros and into the back of the net, giving the Sharks' a 2-0 lead at 16:10 in the first. The Sharks nearly added their third of the night while shorthanded late in the period, but Kiefer Sherwood and Zack Ostapchuk's 2-on-1 was stopped by Saros, who made a big save to deny Ostapchuk.

4:45 into the second period, Ivar Stenberg scored his seventh of the season just moments after he knocked the stick out of Saros' hand with a powerful shot.

Just after the halfway point in the game, Ostapchuk was tripped in a dangerous fashion by Shane Wright and went head first into the boards. Wright was assessed a two-minute minor on the play and Ostapchuk went straight down the tunnel but left under his own power and later returned to the game.

The Predators got on the board with 2:17 remaining in the second period as Nils Hoglander redirected a puck past Nedeljkovic for his fifth goal of the season.

Kiefer Sherwood then went down in the corner in the third period, after slowly skating off the ice, he immediately went to the Sharks' locker room.

After an absurd passing play by the Sharks, Macklin Celebrini was able to score on an empty net as Saros found himself well out of position to make it a 4-1 game and put the final nail in the coffin for the Predators' winning streak.

The Predators did get one back late though, as with 3:25 remaining in the game, Steven Stamkos was able to chip the puck past Nedeljkovic to make it a 4-2 game

The Sharks will look to keep their momentum rolling on Tuesday when they host the Calgary Flames.

#2813. Juice your growth and save on taxes in this workplace investment account

Workers are in the heart of open enrollment season for next year's benefits, and rising healthcare costs are shining a spotlight on an offering that includes a tax-advantaged investment account.

Healthcare expenses are climbing for workers and employers alike, with average total family premiums, including the employer's share, averaging $28,303, up 5% from a year ago, according to data from health policy organization KFF, formerly the Kaiser Family Foundation.

To manage the costs, a growing number of employers are moving to high-deductible health plans, which work alongside health savings accounts or HSAs. Almost 40% of companies with at least 10 employees that offer health benefits also have an HSA-qualified plan, KFF found.

Health savings accounts have three major tax advantages. First, contributions that your employer automatically deducts from your pay aren't taxed. Second, money stashed into the HSA grows free of taxes. Finally, you can make tax-free withdrawals for qualified health expenses – just be sure to save your receipts.

"I love HSAs because of their triple-tax advantage," said Eileen Olson, CFP and founder of Emerald Wealth in Daufuskie Island, South Carolina, near Savannah, Georgia. "For people who can afford to leave it alone, it can be one of the best retirement accounts they're going to have."

In 2027, those with individual healthcare coverage through a high-deductible plan can stash up to $4,500 in an HSA, including any contributions from employers. That number goes up to $9,000 for family plans. Individuals can kick in a catch-up contribution of $1,000 if they're aged 55 and older and not enrolled in Medicare.

The HSA differs from the healthcare flexible spending account, known as the FSA.

FSA funds must be used by the end of the plan year unless your employer allows a carryover of up to $700 or an additional 2.5 months to use the money. HSAs, however, have no expiration date on their funds – and that's what makes them such a powerful tool for long-term planning. You can also take the HSA with you if you change employers.

"Get the money deployed, take advantage of the pretax contribution, and allocate the money into a growth portfolio," said Mark Stancato, certified financial planner at VIP Wealth Advisors in Decatur, Georgia. "With the HSA, you can keep compounding year over year."

The structure of an HSA and the available investment menu will vary from one plan to the next, and some will require that employees keep some of their holdings in cash.

Preset HSA fund menus tend to have 15 to 25 funds to choose from, and providers say that accountholders who invest tend to use two to four funds, according to Greg Carlson, senior research analyst for equity strategies at Morningstar.

How you allocate your HSA will depend on several factors, including your family's health prospects in the next year, your cash flow needs – how much you can comfortably sock into the HSA – and your risk tolerance, said André Small, CFP and founder of A Small Investment in Houston.

Small's standard is to keep an amount equal to 120% of the plan's out-of-pocket maximum held in cash in the HSA.

"That covers a bad year without forcing a sale at the wrong time," he said. "Above that amount? I typically advise investing the amount the client is comfortable investing, allocated to their risk tolerance, their time horizon and the goal of spending these dollars on care later."

For 2027, the IRS defines a high-deductible plan as one with an annual deductible no less than $1,750 for self-only coverage ($3,500 for family plans) and with an out-of-pocket expense limit that doesn't exceed $8,700 for self-only (or $17,400 for family coverage). Out-of-pocket expenses include deductibles, copays and other costs – but not premiums.

Other advisors suggest having up to the deductible in cash, though some HSA plan providers require a stated minimum amount in cash.

Beyond this cash reserve, financial advisors recommend allocating into stocks.

Patrick Swift, CFP and president of Amplius Wealth Advisors in Blue Bell, Pennsylvania, aims for an allocation of up to 60% in U.S. large cap stocks, 20% in small and mid-cap stocks and 20% in international stocks.

"We believe the equity portfolio should be well diversified," he said. "If the [HSA] balance is 2% to 10% of your net worth, why not be aggressive? That can change based on the situation, but if you are comfortable with the philosophical aspect, why not be growth oriented?"

Similarly, Stancato likes to go heavy on equity exposure in HSA accounts. "I allocate more dollars into small caps and emerging markets – those asset classes with more volatility but also more opportunity for growth," the advisor said. He also adds some large-cap domestic exposure.

For the adventurous, select HSA providers are offering brokerage window access, meaning investors can pick from a broader array of investments, including individual stocks.

This year, seven providers surveyed by Morningstar offer access to brokerage windows – up from four in 2023 – including HealthEquity, Fidelity and Lively. Be aware that there may be annual fees attached, either a flat dollar amount or a percentage of assets.

Rachel Elson, a CFP and wealth advisor at Perigon Wealth Management in San Francisco, recommends keeping things simple. "You can do a three-ETF model, covering some allocation that includes the U.S. market, and two ETFs for developed and emerging markets."

"You don't need to get super complicated here," she added.

#2812. Mike Tomlin says Steelers 'got smashed, ran into a buzzsaw' vs Bengals

The Cincinnati Bengals made an emphatic statement while securing first place in the AFC North with a 38-18 rout of the Pittsburgh Steelers in their NFL Week 10 showdown on NBC's Sunday Night Football at Paycor Stadium.

Bengals quarterback Joe Burrow had 409 passing yards - including 302 in the first half - with four touchdowns before giving way to Joe Flacco. Receiver Ja'Marr Chase had the fourth 200+-yard game of his remarkable career.

The first half was particularly ugly for Pittsburgh. NBC Sports analyst and former Bengals receiver Cris Collinsworth said early in the third quarter that he's never seen the Steelers humiliated the way they were in the first half.

"They got smashed tonight," analyst and former Steelers head coach Mike Tomlin said. "They ran into a buzzsaw."

The bad? Offensive linemen Dalton Risner and Dylan Fairchild left the game with injuries.

There was a missed call early. Evan McPherson's Bengals-record field-goal streak ended at 25, and Jordan Battle had a taunting penalty that aroused the ire of Collinsworth.

#2811. 'Genius' batted ball on kick return helps Seahawks burn clock, sparks officiating confusion in win over Raiders

A bizarre play that the CBS broadcast hailed as "genius" and drew a pat on the back from head coach Mike Macdonald helped the Seattle Seahawks run out the clock on their 21-13 win over the Las Vegas Raiders on Sunday.

It also sparked officiating confusion that remained unsettled postgame.

The play in question took place on a Las Vegas kickoff with 2:04 remaining and Seattle holding a 21-13 lead. Matt Gay's kick appeared destined to go out of the end zone for a touchback with no time taken off the clock that would have given Seattle the ball at the 35-yard line.

But Tory Horton had different ideas. The Seahawks return man leapt and batted the ball toward the field of play before it could sail out of bounds. He then instructed Rashid Shaheed to pick it up and return it. Shaheed obliged, and when he was tackled at the 19-yard line, the game clock had run down to 1:59, inside the 2-minute warning.

When the play was done, Macdonald greeted Horton with a smile, a high-five and a pat on the back.

Horton's decision to keep the ball in play cost Seattle 16 yards. More importantly, it eliminated the 2-minute warning from Seattle's ensuing offensive possession. It essentially robbed the Raiders of a valuable de facto timeout.

The Seahawks went on to get a first down and kneel out the clock on the win in victory formation. Even if they hadn't managed a first down, Las Vegas, which had two timeouts remaining, wouldn't have been able to get the ball back with any substantial time on the clock thanks to Horton's play. His decision and Shaheed's execution essentially eliminated 40 seconds of game clock.

After the game, Horton discussed his decision with reporters and what was discussed with coaches prior to to the kick.

"All he said was keep the ball in play, field every return, just don't let it go out," Horton said, apparently referring to special teams coach Jay Harbaugh. "Make sure we don't kneel it in the end zone."

From there, the situational awareness and decision-making was all on Horton.

"I took into consideration, I'm gonna try by any means keep this ball in play so we can get a return started. … I made sure I knew where I was on the field before I leaped up, made sure I was in bounds taking off.

"It's just the situational awareness of like knowing the distance and the time and the situation of the game of knowing, all right, this is a must return. .. I just wanted to make sure I made the smartest play for us."

The legality of the play wasn't immediately clear. Officials conferred at the play's conclusion and held a lengthy discussion before addressing the crowd.

After meeting with the Raiders' sideline, referee Bill Vinovich offered this explanation that didn't exactly provide clarity:

CBS rules analyst Gene Steratore was left perplexed after watching the ordeal and didn't agree that a penalty had actually occurred.

"It sounds to me like Bill is saying that there was maybe a foul," Steratore said. "I don't think it's a foul. It's a kick that never hits the ground. I believe you can bat a kick in the air, provided it has never hit the ground yet."

Per the game's official play-by-play, the play was, indeed, ruled an "illegal bat." And the Raiders declined the penalty, since accepting it wouldn't have put time back on the clock.

Here's the definition of an illegal bat, per section 12-5-1 of the NFL rulebook:

any player bats or punches a loose ball in the field of play toward his opponent's goal line;

any player bats or punches a loose ball (that has touched the ground) in any direction, if it is in either end zone;

an offensive player bats a backward pass in flight toward his opponent's goal line

a forward pass that is controlled by a player prior to completing the catch is thrown forward (the ball remains alive if caught by a teammate or intercepted).

Exception: A forward pass in flight may be tipped, batted, or deflected in any direction by any eligible player at any time.

Is the ball in the "field of play" when it's sailing out of bound without having touched the ground on a kickoff? If so, the bat would appear to be illegal per subsection 1 above. If not, officials got it wrong.

Steratore appeared to have referenced subsection 2, which declares the bat of a ball "in either end zone" illegal when the ball "has touched the ground." The ball hadn't touched the ground, so this would not apply to Horton's play in determining that the bat was illegal.

The rulebook defines the "field of play" as "the area bounded by goal lines and sidelines." It goes on to define the end zones separately as "the areas bounded by goal lines, end lines, and sidelines." This appears to add up to Horton's play having legally taken place outside the "field of play" and officials getting it wrong.

Even if the Raiders had accepted the penalty, time wasn't going to be put back on the clock. The goal of pushing the clock under the 2-minute warning was accomplished, and it turned out to be a heads-up play by Horton that few if anybody watching Sunday had seen before — legal or not.

#2809. Heating oil prices are up 59% from last winter. The spike is 'crushing folks,' Maine nonprofit says

Winter heating bills can strain household budgets. This year, a spike in heating oil prices could make the season especially difficult for households that depend on the fuel and have little financial wiggle room.

The national average price of heating oil was roughly $5.91 a gallon as of Nov. 9, up about $2.19, or 59%, from a year earlier, according to the U.S. Energy Information Administration.

In Maine, the average price per gallon is roughly $5.83, up about $2.49 from a year earlier, or about 75%. The state is among those whose residents may feel the price rise acutely — about 50% of homes there use heating oil.

The jump "is crushing folks," said Jason Parent, CEO of AVEC Community Action, a nonprofit agency based in Presque Isle, Maine, that provides heating assistance and other social services in three of the state's 16 counties. Many residents face immediate crises, he said, such as empty fuel tanks or reliance on unsafe heating sources.

"My fear is that there are households out there who have secondary fuel sources like wood stoves, pellet stoves and other things that are no longer operating and functioning in a safe way," Parent said.

The price surge comes on the heels of years of high overall inflation that has already squeezed household budgets, he said.

"The cost of living has made it impossible for households who had been figuring out how to get by without [financial help]," Parent said.

The price spike comes amid a tight global market for refined fuels, a category that includes heating oil and diesel. Supply shocks tied to the Iran war and the Russia-Ukraine war, low inventories and limited refining capacity have constrained supplies and added upward pressure on fuel prices, according to recent research from the Federal Reserve Bank of Kansas City.

The EIA predicts that a warmer winter in the Northeast will reduce consumption, helping to offset higher prices. In October, the agency forecast that the average household using heating oil will spend $2,115 from November through March, about 21% more than last year.

Meanwhile, homes primarily heated with natural gas are expected to spend an average of $640 this winter, down 9%, while those using propane are forecast to spend an average of $1,246, down 3%, according to the EIA's October forecast. Households using electricity for heating are expected to spend an average of $1,196, up 4% from a year ago. These are national figures.

Roughly 4.1 million U.S. households use heating oil as their primary means of heating their homes, according to the EIA. Most of them — close to 3.4 million — are located in the Northeast.

Unlike electricity or natural gas, heating oil is delivered in large quantities. Payment terms vary, but some dealers require a minimum delivery or charge extra for smaller orders. In Maine, for example, ordering at least 100 gallons for a standard 275-gallon tank can avoid a surcharge, according to the state attorney general's office. At current prices, that 100 gallons would cost about $600 — a tall order for some households.

AVEC's Home Energy Assistance Program, or HEAP, provides financial assistance to qualifying households, based primarily on income and the number of people living in the home. Funding originates with the federal Low Income Home Energy Assistance Program, or LIHEAP, which is run by the U.S. Health and Human Services Department and overseen by state agencies, often working with local organizations such as AVEC.

Maine and tribal grantees in the state received about $38 million from LIHEAP in October, according to a press release from outgoing Republican Sen. Susan Collins. AVEC administers about $4 million of that through HEAP, Parent said. AVEC also gets donations from community fundraising to help residents cover heating costs.

A component of HEAP is the Energy Crisis Intervention Program, which serves qualifying families who face an energy crisis, such as a potential electric shutoff or an empty oil tank and no financial means to replenish the supply.

AVEC began taking calls for that program on Nov. 2, Parent said.

"In the first hour alone, there were 150 voicemail messages on our emergency fuel line," Parent said. "People were in crisis before the program opened up."

Catherine Lafleche of Hersey, Maine, describes AVEC as a lifeline.

The 65-year-old is guardian to two grandchildren, ages 11 and 6, and is living mostly on about $1,100 in monthly Social Security benefits and state guardianship assistance, after shattering her upper-arm bone in a fall in April. Her injury required reverse shoulder replacement surgery, from which she continues to recover. Before that fall, she had worked as a substitute teacher and bookkeeper.

"I'm $6,000 short of income at this point in the year," Lafleche said.

Hersey is in Maine's northernmost county, Aroostook, where January and February bring average high temperatures in the 20s and average lows in the single digits, based on National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration data from 1991 to 2020 recorded at a Caribou Municipal Airport weather station. Last winter, the area experienced severe cold snaps, including on Jan. 24, when the high was minus 2 degrees Fahrenheit.

Lafleche applied to HEAP through AVEC in August, but a backlog meant potentially waiting until November for assistance. Unable to afford more fuel when it became chilly, she ran out of heating oil in early October and relied on a small wood stove to keep part of her house warm, burning Amazon boxes collected from other local residents because she didn't have enough firewood.

In late October, an AVEC representative called to say a charitable donation would cover 100 gallons of heating oil.

"When the oil man came, I cried," Lafleche said.

For residents in Maine who need help covering the cost to heat their homes and are seeking out help, "the need is outpacing the benefits," said Parent, of AVEC.

The average benefit through HEAP is about $750 per household this season, up from roughly $550 a year ago, Parent said. "That $750 is going less far than the $550 did last season" for heating oil, he said. "It's buying fewer gallons because of the precipitous increase."

The benefits are generally paid directly to the fuel supplier as a credit on the household's account. Even if a household qualifies, funding may run out.

"We're in a world of hurt now ... but it's going to be crisis time again in January or February," Parent said.

The Trump administration, in an effort to bring down heating oil prices, said it would release 300,000 barrels of heating oil from the Northeast Home Heating Oil Reserve, according to a Nov. 4 release from the U.S. Energy Department.

Whether adding this extra heating oil to the market will reduce prices for residents depends at least partly on how it gets doled out, said Jonathan Rubin, director of the Margaret Chase Smith Policy Center and a professor of economics at the University of Maine.

"No one really knows the economic impact, because will the home-heating oil distributors release it in one month, which would make it about 10% to 12% of a single month's supply? Or will they release it over the entire season, in which case it's only about one and a half percent of the entire season's home heating oil supply?" Rubin said.

"I would say that this is a modest short-term buffer," he said.

Congressional lawmakers have called for increased LIHEAP funding this year due to rising energy costs, including heating oil. Dozens of House lawmakers — mostly Democrats — signed an Oct. 14 letter to the House Appropriations Committee seeking an additional $3 billion in emergency supplemental funding for LIHEAP.

That followed an Oct. 1 letter from a group of Democratic senators and Sen. Bernie Sanders, I-Vt., to Robert F. Kennedy Jr., secretary of the Health and Human Services, also asking that he seek at least $3 billion more for LIHEAP.

Already, the government released roughly $3.63 billion in LIHEAP funding nationwide on Oct. 9 — 90% of the roughly $4 billion available under a continuing resolution that is currently funding federal agencies.

The timing for disbursing the remainder depends on when Congress either passes full-year funding for the 2027 fiscal year — which started Oct. 1 and runs through Sept. 30, 2027 — or passes another stopgap measure to keep the government running. If lawmakers do neither by Dec. 11, a federal shutdown would begin.

Whether the additional $3 billion requested for LIHEAP will be considered in any upcoming congressional appropriations process is uncertain.

Lafleche, the Hersey resident, said the 100 gallons of heating oil donated to her, along with some firewood she has now purchased, should keep her home warm until early December. She hopes to receive approval for benefits by then through HEAP, which has helped her in past years.

"We're not in this position by choice. Most of us made every plan to the contrary, but it stacked up to be not enough. And I feel like we don't deserve to be cold," she said.

#2808. 32 things we learned in NFL Week 10: Trade deadline already delivering big benefits

The 32 things we learned from Week 10 of the 2026 NFL season:

0. The still-winless Los Angeles Chargers remain on pace to become the NFL's first-ever 0-17 team … unless they can notch their first victory of 2026 in prime time on Monday night in Baltimore, when Jacoby Brissett is set to make his debut as the Ravens' latest QB1.

1. The number of games every NFL team – save the Chicago Bears, Denver Broncos, Philadelphia Eagles and Tampa Bay Buccaneers, who were all on their bye week – has played since the Nov. 10 trade deadline. And those that delved into the market experienced almost immediate returns.

2. Once and present and future Minnesota Vikings QB Kirk Cousins only needed five days to get his former and current team back on track – leading the Vikes to a resounding 30-10 victory over the Green Bay Packers just six days after they were embarrassed by the Buffalo Bills on the "Monday Night Football" prime-time platform.

2a. And it was a meaningful win for an emotional Cousins, whose previous game with the Vikings had also been at Lambeau Field – one during which he tore his Achilles tendon in a game midway through a 2023 season that was shaping up as one of the best years of his career up to that point. Minnesota did not re-sign Cousins in 2024, and he joined the Atlanta Falcons.

3. New Kansas City Chiefs WR Josh Downs seemed to fit into his new circumstances snugly, catching four balls for 53 yards and a TD in his K.C. debut. Downs also assumed the punt return duties for his new team.

4. The number of NFL officials who worked a pair of games Sunday in the aftermath of the FAA's Friday ground stop, which caused travel havoc that rippled through the weekend. Referee Don Willard, umpire Terry Killens, field judge Jabir Walker and side judge Justin Larrew worked the Dolphins-Colts matchup at 1 p.m. ET in Indianapolis, then drove to Cincinnati for the "Sunday Night Football" showdown between the Steelers and Bengals. Willard, normally a side judge, not only was on double duty but in an unfamiliar role to boot.

NFL PLAYOFF PICTURE: Two new teams take over first place in AFC; NFC has new No. 2 seed

4a. The number of TD passes thrown Sunday in Cincinnati by Bengals QB Joe Burrow – the 13th time he's reached that threshold in his career. It's also the fifth consecutive game Burrow has accounted for at least four TDs in a game, clearly putting him at the forefront of the MVP conversation.

5. The number of TD passes thrown Sunday in Atlanta by Chiefs QB Patrick Mahomes – the ninth time, playoffs included, he's reached that threshold in his career but first since Week 1 of the 2022 season.

5a. The number of TD passes thrown Sunday by Dallas Cowboys QB Dak Prescott – the second time, playoffs included, he's reached that threshold in his career … though with far less impact on this day than Mahomes. More on Dallas later.

6. The number of game-winning drives Cleveland Browns QB Deshaun Watson has engineered this season for the 6-4 Browns – i.e., he's done so in all of their games, including Sunday's 27-24 defeat of the Houston Texans, Watson's original club. Cleveland trailed 17-0 early in the second quarter.

6a. Also the number of NFL games the league staged in Europe this season, Sunday's matchup between the Detroit Lions and New England Patriots in Munich the final installment of that sextet.

7. Lions WR Amon-Ra St. Brown, whose mother is German, shone in the contest – which wasn't quite a homecoming for a man who has Deutschland citizenship, but close. He had his first multi-TD game since Week 2 in Detroit's 35-10 blowout victory. "Being in Germany, number one, was a blessing, it's been amazing," said St. Brown afterward, who answered many questions in recent days in German. "But being able to win like that − with the guys, my teammates in front of the fans here in Germany, it's like a dream come true."

8. The number of times Packers QB Jordan Love was sacked Sunday by the blitz-happy Vikings defense. Prior to that nightmare, Love had never been bagged more than five times in an NFL game.

9. Pretty cool that Jacksonville Jaguars head coach gave his new acquisitions, DT Jordan Davis and S Jessie Bates III, Sunday off given neither has had a bye week nor was in line for one given the Jags' remaining schedule.

10. And Jacksonville didn't need its new guys, rolling the Titans 40-23 – the Jaguars' fifth win this season by at least 17 points.

11. We'll know in two months whether or not the Jags overpaid for Bates and Davis, who will cost the franchise one first- and two second-round picks. But the 2026 squad's trajectory was, and continues to be, impressive.

12. While the Cousins deal worked as intended – so far – for Minnesota, Las Vegas Raiders rookie QB Fernando Mendoza maybe wasn't as ready to start as reports coming out of the desert suggested. He had some promising early moments Sunday against the Seattle Seahawks' "Dark Side" defense, but the Silver and Black wound up with fewer than 100 net passing yards in the first regular-season start for the No. 1 pick of the 2026 draft – Mendoza sacked six times for 45 yards in losses … and he didn't seem to see some of the pressure coming at him.

13. The number of penalties committed by the Texans on Sunday, costing them 112 yards. Bad teams, talented or not, find ways to beat themselves.

14. The jersey number of Seattle QB Sam Darnold, who's missed the past two games with various injuries. Sunday, backup QB Drew Lock improved to 3-0 this season and also shepherded the team to victory in Week 1, when Darnold was also hurt. Lock's wins have come against the Arizona Cardinals (twice) and Las Vegas Raiders, but you have to wonder if, at some point, the Seahawks opt for the hot hand. With their bye upcoming, they have another week to decide as Darnold continues to heal.

15. The jersey number of Seahawks WR-KR Tory Horton, who made one of the more heads-up special teams plays you'll ever see in a bid to get his team past the two-minute warning after the Raiders had pulled within one score but had to kick off with 2:04 left on the clock. He leapt high to swat the incoming kickoff forward and then directed teammate Rashid Shaheed to return it and get Seattle inside of the two-minute mark. Horton was flagged for illegally batting the ball on the very unique sequence, but Las Vegas declined it. Regardless, his play achieved its objective, and Seattle prevailed 21-13.

16. Lock completed 14 of 17 passes in Sin City for 240 yards and two TDs, giving him a nearly perfect 158.0 passer rating. However, he did fumble twice, turnovers the same bugaboo that has plagued Darnold.

17. The number of penalties committed by the Carolina Panthers on Sunday, costing them 152 yards – both franchise records. Bad teams, talented or not, find ways to beat themselves – though not necessarily in the abhorrent NFC South, Carolina surviving the New Orleans Saints 20-17 on the road.

18. Thankfully for the Panthers, they still have RB Chuba Hubbard, one of the league's more underrated backs. His 172 yards from scrimmage Sunday represented 61% of Carolina's offensive output. His two TDs similarly provided 60% of his team's points.

19. The Buffalo Bills got seven tackles and a pass defended by new S Amani Hooker, a player the Tennessee Titans were willing to part with.

20. And though you hate to glean too much from wins over the New York Jets (on Sunday), Vikings and Ravens – all teams the Bills have caught without their QB1s for at least a portion of those matchups – Buffalo has allowed just 28 points since its Week 7 bye. The AFC East leaders, which surrendered at least 29 points in five of their first six games this season, have permitted just one touchdown – total – in its past three games.

21. We'll continue to wonder why the Indianapolis Colts didn't trade RB Jonathan Taylor – though, apparently, they didn't get the Godfather offer they wanted, Round 2 draft pick compensation evidently insufficient.

22. Yet the Colts, who are clearly headed into rebuild with their soon-to-be 28-year-old bell cow under contract through the 2028 season, continue to seemingly manage his touches. Taylor had 18 in Sunday's 24-22 defeat of the Miami Dolphins, his third straight game with fewer than 20. (He had at least 20 in six of Indy's first seven game this season.) Maybe Indy is hoping its next GM can trade a fresh Taylor for more draft capital in a few months.

23. We'll also continue to wonder why the Titans didn't trade RB Tony Pollard, who scored his sixth TD in the past three games Sunday but likely only has eight games left with one-win Tennessee before he leaves in free agency. We'll see if the 2028 compensatory pick the franchise might get will be worth whatever it could have reaped for the versatile back, who will turn 30 in the offseason.

24. Los Angeles Rams legend Aaron Donald began Sunday still seeking his first post-unretirement sack. But he got Arizona Cardinals QB Carson Beck 1½ times in the rookie's first regular-season start.

25. And the injury-depleted Rams won their fourth in a row and have rushed for at least 120 yards in each of those victories – including a season-high 202 on Sunday.

26. The San Francisco 49ers' 46-40 defeat of Dallas was more convincing than the final score indicates.

27. The Niners' 584 yards were the most ever piled up against the Cowboys in their own building and, per Elias, San Francisco's three TDs from 60+ yards were tied for the most in a game by one team over the past half-century.

28. Christian McCaffrey's 66- and 74-yard TD catches – mostly runs after the catch – were the two longest of his 10-year career. They also gave him the league's career record for most TD receptions by a running back.

29. And San Francisco, which had already reacquired DT DeForest Buckner, got a nice initial ROI from its latest trade deadline acquisition – WR Keenan Allen catching five balls for 61 yards in his 49ers debut after coming over from the Colts just days before.

30. Is monochrome black slimming? Maybe in the win column. Just ask the Saints.

31. Is monochrome black slimming? Maybe in the win column. Just ask the Jets.

32. Not much to like about the Cowboys this season. But we appreciate them honoring veterans and Medal of Honor winners annually at this time of year by adding a red stripe to the centerline of their helmets as part of the NFL's "Salute to Service" program.

#2807. Mike Tomlin says Steelers 'got smashed, ran into a buzzsaw' vs Bengals

The Cincinnati Bengals made an emphatic statement while securing first place in the AFC North with a 38-18 rout of the Pittsburgh Steelers in their NFL Week 10 showdown on NBC's Sunday Night Football at Paycor Stadium.

Bengals quarterback Joe Burrow had 409 passing yards - including 302 in the first half - with four touchdowns before giving way to Joe Flacco. Receiver Ja'Marr Chase had the fourth 200+-yard game of his remarkable career.

The first half was particularly ugly for Pittsburgh. NBC Sports analyst and former Bengals receiver Cris Collinsworth said early in the third quarter that he's never seen the Steelers humiliated the way they were in the first half.

"They got smashed tonight," analyst and former Steelers head coach Mike Tomlin said. "They ran into a buzzsaw."

The bad? Offensive linemen Dalton Risner and Dylan Fairchild left the game with injuries.

There was a missed call early. Evan McPherson's Bengals-record field-goal streak ended at 25, and Jordan Battle had a taunting penalty that aroused the ire of Collinsworth.

#2805. Robinhood shares fall as crypto slump weighs on profits, offsetting gains in new businesses

Robinhood shares were down around 8% in pre-market trading on Wednesday as investors digested weaker-than-expected third-quarter earnings despite growth in options trading and prediction markets.

Here's how the brokerage performed in its quarter ended Sept. 30, compared with Wall Street estimates, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

Robinhood's net income fell to $468 million, or 51 cents per share, from $556 million, or 61 cents per share, a year ago. Revenue represented a 12% increase from $1.27 billion in the same quarter last year.

Despite that gain, transaction-based revenue of $867 million came in below Wall Street estimates from StreetAccount of $879 million, putting focus on whether Robinhood can sustain momentum across its trading businesses and reduce its reliance on volatile market activity.

Robinhood has been diversifying its business as it tries to expand its brokerage app into a broader financial services platform. Options trading and event contracts have become increasingly important sources of transaction revenue, while the company has continued to invest in wealth management, subscriptions and other offerings.

Transaction-based revenue grew 19% from the same period last year to $867 million, driven primarily by a more than 500% increase in event contracts revenue. Additionally, options revenue grew 31% to $399 million and equities revenue rose 41% to $121 million.

Those gains were partially offset by cryptocurrency revenue, which fell 55% to $120 million.

Chief Financial Officer Shiv Verma told CNBC event contracts on crypto movements did well in the quarter, offering investors another way to express a view on crypto despite the slump in prices and spot trading.

Net deposits on the Robinhood platform totaled $15.7 billion for the quarter, equal to a 17% annualized growth rate based on total platform assets at the end of Q2 2026. Over the past year, net deposits totaled $71 billion, representing 21% growth compared with total platform assets at the end of Q3 2025.

—With reporting from CNBC's Robert Hum and Kate Rooney

#2804. I'm a psychologist who studies couples—5 signs you've fallen into a common rut: 'It builds through small habits that each look harmless'

Couples today, especially ones who've been together for many years, are vulnerable to a silent, almost invisible and slow-growing "relationship disease." I call this condition coexistence without contact. Couples living with this "disease" share a home, a bed and a schedule while living in almost entirely separate daily realities.

In her book "Reclaiming Conversation," for example, MIT sociologist Sherry Turkle describes the modern experience of "being together, but also being elsewhere," and argues that it undermines our capacity to have the conversations that count. Real conversations require both people to be present.

As a psychologist who specializes in relationships, I see couples having fewer of those real conversations and being less present with each other. They build systems for running a shared life. They work out who handles the mornings, who books the appointments, which chores belong to whom and how the week gets planned. These systems are a genuine achievement, and they're indicative of good management.

But a relationship isn't a company you're trying to run. A company improves when you divide responsibilities clearly and let each person own one area. A relationship is more complicated than that. In my experience, what holds two people together is overlap, along with conversation and genuine connection.

It builds through small habits that each look harmless, but get out of hand because you repeat them for a decade. In my experience, three of them do most of the damage:

In my experience, this issue shows up in five fairly predictable ways.

You confirm the plumber is coming Tuesday, agree on who's collecting your daughter from band practice and decide what to do about your mother's birthday.

But while you settle things and close loops, you never get to the strange thing a colleague said in a meeting, the article you read on the train or the fact that you've been contemplating leaving your job. These tangents you're missing are how you keep learning about a person you already know well.

Looking at your phone can feel easier than starting a conversation. So you head to bed at different times, scroll for 20 minutes and turn the lights off without saying anything.

That window when you unwind together and talk before falling asleep matters because being tired can lower your filter. People say things at 11 p.m. that they might not have shared at 8 p.m.

You both know about the dentist on Thursday and the school thing on Friday because the calendar told you. Mornings become a matter of getting everyone out of the house on time, and you leave knowing your partner's schedule without knowing what they're dreading in it. A calendar can tell you where your partner will be but not how they feel about being there.

You catch up on errands, admin, repairs and the people you owe a visit. Against a backlog like that, splitting up is simply the better strategy, since two people working separately cover far more ground than two people doing the same job together.

But you've already spent five days mostly apart, and the weekend is sometimes the only real opportunity to close that gap. An idle Saturday might look like waste, but an "unproductive" weekend is sometimes exactly what a relationship needs.

It's easy enough to go your separate ways whenever you do have downtime. You have your shows, your partner has theirs and the one you watch together is a compromise neither of you is enthusiastic about.

Couples tend to read this as a sign they've grown incompatible, when it's usually the result of spending years avoiding accommodating each other's taste. That negotiation keeps each of you current on what the other actually enjoys now, which changes more over a decade than people expect.

It also gives you regular practice at setting your own preference aside for someone else's, a skill that goes rusty without use. Couples who stop exercising it on something as low-stakes as a Friday evening often find it much harder to manage when the stakes are real.

The good news is that each bit of contact you get back makes the next one easier to reach. Here are a few places to start:

Mark Travers, Ph.D., is a psychologist who specializes in relationships. He holds degrees from Cornell University and the University of Colorado Boulder. He is the lead psychologist at Awake Therapy, a telehealth company that provides online psychotherapy, counseling and coaching. He is also the curator of the popular mental health and wellness website Therapytips.org.

#2803. 'Root cause’ of nationwide ground stop is unknown, FAA says

The root cause of the longest halt in flight departures to U.S. airports since 9/11 remains unclear after a Saturday press conference by U.S. transportation officials.

Routers and networking equipment appeared to be involved in the technological disruption that grounded flights across the country, but a definitive cause has not been established as of Saturday afternoon.

The inability to pinpoint the reason for the infrastructure malfunctions led the Federal Aviation Administration to take actions that resulted in the grounding order, Bryan Bedford, administrator of the Federal Aviation Administration, told reporters.

"Because of our lack of identifying the root cause, we were unwilling to take any risk that this propagation could actually result in a sensor losing connectivity with aircraft," he said.

"What we saw was instability. We couldn't explain the instability," Bedford added.

Officials reported issues with routers and networking equipment on Saturday, citing products and services from Cisco and L3Harris Technologies.

"Until I can build [the new architecture], I'm stuck with L3Harris, who owns this network," Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy said.

In a statement on X, L3Harris said that it, in partnership with other tech companies, has completed more than 70% of the planned airspace communications conversion from legacy copper wires to a fiber-optic system.

"We are supporting the FAA in its investigation to understand the conditions that led to the Nov. 13 ground stop of our nation's air travel," the statement said. "L3Harris takes the nationwide ground stop and its impact on travelers, airlines and air traffic operations seriously."

On Friday, Duffy and Department of Homeland Security Secretary Markwayne Mullin called for an air travel overhaul, with Duffy requesting $50 billion from Congress.

FAA administrator Bedford said that router updates were a matter of regular business for his agency and distinct from modernization allocations.

"We are constantly replacing 2,000, you know, Cisco 600 routers with Juniper routers, right? So this is ordinary course of business stuff. This is not part of the … FAA $12.5 billion modernization efforts. So let's not conflate those two things," Bedford said.

#2800. LeBron James climbs to third on NBA all-time assists list, passing Jason Kidd

The NBA icon passed Jason Kidd to move into third for most assists all time in pro basketball. He did so during the Philadelphia 76ers 131-124 win over the Indiana Pacers on Sunday, Nov. 15 at Xfinity Mobile Arena in Philadelphia.

James' 12,092nd assist came with 1:19 left in the fourth quarter. Dean Wade passed the ball to James, who was standing near the free throw line. James quickly tossed the ball up to VJ Edgecombe, who took two steps under the basket and slammed a dunk.

James spoke on how passing the ball has been a foundational part of his game from the beginning. He is now playing in his 24th season, an NBA record.

"From the moment I picked up a basketball when I first started playing like recreational basketball, five-on-five, I always got a joy and a kick out of seeing my teammates score," he said on the NBC Sports broadcast after the win. "It was a feeling that felt really good about me being able to help one of my teammates out. ... Yeah, I got a kick out of that from day one."

James needs 461 assists to pass Chris Paul, who sits in second place on the assists list with 12,552. John Stockton holds the NBA record with 15,806 assists. James is already the NBA's all-time leading points scorer.

"It means I had a lot of great teammates," James said of reaching the assists mark. "You gotta have teammates that can put the ball in the hole. My job is just try to put it on time, on target. Lead 'em to the hole either from layups, dunks, threes, midrange, whatever the case may be.

"Any time I'm on any list in NBA history, I love the history of the game. I love the players that came before me. It's super honorable."

The landmark assist was one of 11 that James had on the night. He added 15 points and seven rebounds to his stat line. Jaylen Brown led Philadelphia with 31 points, two rebounds and two assists. Tyrese Maxey added 27 points, four rebounds and six assists.

Pascal Siakam had 31 points, two rebounds and two assists for Indiana in the loss.

James joined the 76ers this offseason after leaving the Los Angeles Lakers. In his time in Philadelphia so far this season, James beat the defending champion New York Knicks in the season opener and helped with a 29-point comeback against his hometown team, the Cleveland Cavaliers.

This article originally appeared on USA TODAY: LeBron James climbs to third on NBA all-time assists list, passing Jason Kidd

#2799. How Nvidia turmoil and a Democratic Congress could reshape the AI trade, according to ETF experts

The largest technology stocks linked to the artificial intelligence trade could stand to benefit the most from Nvidia's woes and a Democratic-controlled Congress pushing back on data centers, according to investing experts interviewed on CNBC's "ETF Edge" earlier this week.

"We may have been put in an environment, as much as investors have sort of been looking for areas outside of Big Tech, where net-net Big Tech ends up as the winner once again," said Roundhill Investments CEO Dave Mazza, whose firm manages the Magnificent Seven ETF (MAGS).

Part of his bull case: Other players in the AI trade are positioned to fill Nvidia's chip demand if investors lose trust in the company after a tumultuous week that saw its iconic founder and CEO Jensen Huang resign.

"If we get into an environment where people can't trust … Nvidia, they're going to look for other sources for these chips because they need them," Mazza said during the "ETF Edge" interview, which occurred Monday as the Nvidia scandal grew but before Huang had resigned.

Discussing recent fund flow activity for the MAGS ETF, as well as for the Roundhill Neocloud ETF (NCLD) — which focuses on smaller market cap, specialized cloud computing companies — Mazza said, "In a weird way, [hyperscalers] may continue to benefit from this because they have the compute available. It's going to be more valuable if the demand is there."

The Roundhill Magnificent Seven ETF ended the week in the green, albeit with a gain of less than 1%, while the Roundhill Neocloud ETF was up over 1%, though earnings from top holdings Coreweave and Nebius presented a mixed picture.

CoreWeave had its best day since April 2025 on Wednesday after its latest quarterly results, while Nebius shares sank after its Thursday report. The neocloud ETF remains deeply in the red since its August 2026 launch, down 39%, amid concerns about balance sheet strength, which could linger given Nvidia's role in financing the AI buildout. CoreWeave shares sank again on Friday, and Nvidia posted its longest losing streak since 2012 after its latest weekly losses.

Nvidia's interim CEO and current chief financial officer Colette Kress will have her first opportunity to reassure the market when Nvidia reports its third-quarter earnings next Tuesday. "Our focus remains on serving our customers, partners, developers and shareholders," Kress said in a statement issued on Thursday morning when the company announced Huang's exit.

Yorkville Ives partner and senior managing director Dan Ives said on "ETF Edge" on Monday that the market will remain optimistic about other AI chip players, like AMD and Alphabet, if Nvidia faces prolonged headwinds. Ives helped create the Dan IVES Wedbush AI Revolution ETF (IVES) while at Wedbush Securities, where he served as the managing director and global head of technology research before departing Wedbush to form his own firm earlier this year.

AMD finished trading this week up close to 13%, with Broadcom posting similar gains.

The tech-heavy Nasdaq index posted its best week since August as Nvidia's slump reshuffled the AI trade.

Yorkville Ives lowered its price target on Nvidia to $225 from $300 on Thursday after Huang's resignation, but maintained a buy rating on the stock, which finished the week at $162.85 per share.

"Nvidia remains the clear leader in GPU chips for the AI Revolution. They are roughly 2-3 years ahead of the nearest competitor and the demand trajectory the next few years is not changing because Jensen is gone," Ives wrote in a note to investors on Thursday. "The strategic roadmap is already essentially built around the next few family of chips and physical AI with 42k employees driving it. ... Valuation at roughly 10x 28 EPS is way too cheap once Nvidia gets through this storm and emerges on the other side."

The compute that does exist could become even more indispensable against the backdrop of a potential slowdown to the data-center buildout by Congress after the Democratic Party gains of Nov. 3 swept control of Congress, Mazza said.

Ives estimates that the "blue tidal wave" in Congress could mean 10% of planned data centers won't materialize on the low end, and 15%-20% on the high end, but he added that demand still outstrips supply four to one. "The compute is just that much more valuable in the capacity today than it was a week ago. So I could argue just more and more bullish, strong, get stronger," Ives said.

"The game is going to change for Big Tech. It will slow it down. But there's no overbuild, no bubble concerns. And I view that as actually a positive thing," he added.

#2796. Top Wall Street analysts are bullish on these 3 stocks for the long haul

The ongoing earnings season, Middle East tensions and buzz ahead of the U.S. midterm elections have been keeping investors busy. Further, worries about elevated artificial intelligence spending are weighing on investor sentiment.

Investors looking beyond the short-term noise and focusing on long-term returns can track top Wall Street analysts to pick the right stocks, backed by strong research.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

This week's first stock pick is Datadog, an AI-powered observability and security platform. The company will announce its results for the third quarter on Nov. 4. Datadog is seeing strong demand for its offerings, thanks to cloud migration and rapid AI adoption. TipRanks' KPIs tool for DDOG shows the growth in its customer base over the past few years amid increasing market penetration and growing adoption of its products.

Heading into Q3 FY26 earnings, Bank of America analyst Koji Ikeda reiterated a buy rating on Datadog stock and raised his price target to $335 from $305. The analyst noted that industry checks and data indicate that infrastructure software demand is strengthening, with AI increasingly driving usage and growth.

Ikeda called Datadog and Atlassian his top picks, saying that companies that can show that AI is a tailwind to growth could see their valuation rerate, even at premium multiples. He added that the total addressable market for observability is growing faster than anticipated, driven by accelerated growth of enterprise AI and the rapid ramp of consumer AI agents.

The five-star analyst believes that focus will be on revenue growth, which he estimates to be 28.7% for Q3 FY26 versus the Street's consensus of 29.1%. Ikeda thinks that the market is underestimating the growth prospects of DDOG's security products. Meanwhile, he cautioned investors about concentration risk related to large customers.

"We think results will be supportive of our long-term thesis that Datadog is a strong share gainer in the rapidly expanding observability opportunity," said Ikeda.

Ikeda ranks No. 673 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 55% of the time, delivering an average return of 10.9%. See Datadog Statistics on TipRanks.

The next pick, semiconductor giant Nvidia, is also benefiting from AI-driven demand for its products. After attending Nvidia's GTC Berlin conference, Morgan Stanley analyst Joseph Moore reaffirmed a buy rating on NVDA stock with a price target of $300, calling it a top pick in the semiconductor space.

The five-star analyst said that the conference supported his thesis that Nvidia continues to effectively navigate bottlenecks in the supply chain, land/power/shell, and funding. In fact, Moore is "convinced that all 3 aspects are opportunities for NVDA."

While Nvidia's GTC Berlin offered no major updates, Moore noted the conference reflected that the company continues to add to its significant advantages heading into 2027. The analyst said that Vera Rubin is gaining momentum, though details on next year's Rubin Ultra were lighter than expected.

With demand outpacing supply, Moore said that focus is on closing the gap. In this regard, he highlighted the supply chain deals on substrate, packaging and testing. While the analyst continues to view TSMC 3 nm wafer supply as the biggest challenge, he expects it to ease over time.

Moore ranks No. 142 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 59% of the time, delivering an average return of 23.4%. See Nvidia Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Aerospace company SpaceX recently announced solid third-quarter results, with its AI revenue surging to $7.56 billion from $813 million in the prior-year quarter.

Impressed by the results, Needham analyst Ryan Koontz reiterated a buy rating on SPCX stock with a price target of $250. Koontz highlighted that SpaceX's Q3 results significantly exceeded expectations, proving that the company's vertically integrated business model is driving massive scale.

Furthermore, Koontz emphasized the 195.2% sequential growth in SpaceX's AI revenue, fueled by wholesale spot compute deals. He noted management's increasing focus on its AI strategy, given that it is optimistic about adding 10 gigawatts of incremental capacity during 2027.

"With increasing confidence on Starship re-use, we meaningfully increase our estimates," said Koontz.

Koontz ranks No. 357 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 42% of the time, delivering an average return of 23.7%. See SpaceX Technical Analysis on TipRanks.

#2794. Could Budapest be the next digital nomad hotspot?

When mechanical engineering graduate and entrepreneur Lexi Vindisch wanted to run her business from a remote location, she didn't pick a town in North America where she lived. Instead, Budapest was top of her list.

With its mix of grand sights, cafés, cultural events and inexpensive public transportation, the Hungarian capital has become a popular place for digital nomads — people embracing the "work from anywhere" trend that took off during the Covid-19 pandemic.

Based at co-working space Create26 in Budapest, Vindisch runs a domestic cleaning company based in Victoria, Canada, as well as overseeing Lead Machine, a marketing software firm for home service businesses.

Hungary ranked fifth for digital nomads on a 2025 list of countries, behind Spain, the United Arab Emirates, Montenegro and the Bahamas. The index, compiled by visa information website VisaGuide, considered factors such as taxes, income and cost of living.

Vindisch, who has worked in many European and North American cities, but has Hungarian roots, said Budapest feels different. "I've been in locales that are super beautiful, well organized, great facilities, but they lacked a personality ... I've never been able to replicate the feeling that Budapest holds anywhere else," she said.

EU citizens can work remotely from Hungary long-term if they meet certain requirements, while Hungary's White Card scheme, which provides one- or two-year residency permits for digital nomads from outside the EU, requires a net monthly income of at least 3,000 euros ($3,370).

There are around 35 co-working spots in central Budapest, according to the Coworking Hungary Association, several of which are in the bustling District VI, near the fashionable bars and restaurants of the Jewish Quarter. Alongside these hubs, the city offers around 35 serviced offices, including locations from larger providers such as Regus and DBH Flex.

"Budapest has its own vibe," said Áron Levendel, a founder of co-working space Kaptar, which is within view of the city's striking St Stephen's Basilica. "Whoever wants to connect, make friends or meet people, has an easy job. Whoever wants to have fun, learn or get experiences in English, will have the challenge of choosing from hundreds of options every evening," he said.

Levendel and his co-founders are organizational developers who wanted a workplace that was both sociable and professional. In 2010, they came up with the idea for Kaptar (the word means "hive" in Hungarian).

"Gmail was already there, but most of the work was not in the cloud or online yet; people had folders and papers. Digital nomads were not a thing," Levendel told CNBC by email. "Co-working" also wasn't a term in common use at the time, so he called the space a "közösségi iroda," or community office.

Since the Covid-19 pandemic, Kaptar has grown more than 30% every year, according to Levendel, including a "wave" of digital nomads who joined after the pandemic, often arriving initially for a month but staying for up to a year. Overseas members either keep an existing job and work remotely, or they are freelancers who can travel, Levendel said.

Mónika Gyurity opened co-working space Liftoff in 2019. After a career as a television executive, she wanted to expand her digital skills and bring different generations together to learn from each other.

Located in a former elevator factory close to Budapest's central Nyugati station, Liftoff attracts a mix of professionals — including short-term visitors in need of a workspace, as well as remote employees working for international companies. One of Liftoff's team members is also a remote worker who regularly participates in Nomad Cruise voyages, or "workation" trips that combine a conference program with land excursions.

Gyurity expects the digital nomad trend to continue, noting that AI now allows individuals to create and launch services or businesses without the resources of a large organization. "More people will be able to imagine their knowledge, skills and opportunities outside the traditional corporate world," she said.

At the same time, she recommends that those in the corporate world spend time in a co-working environment. "Most people are there because they are trying to solve something," she said. "Every day I meet younger people who are at a very different stage of their lives and careers, use different tools, and approach problems differently. We can learn from each other."

A few streets north of the Hungarian Parliament building and steps from the River Danube sits Kubik, a co-working space where roughly 20% of members are digital nomads working in IT, software development, design or marketing. According to co-owner Éva Mocsár, many of these professionals split their time between Budapest and warmer climes, with some choosing to escape the Hungarian winter in Argentina, before returning in the spring.

As with other co-working hubs, one of Mocsár's primary challenges is recruiting new members, particularly as larger office spaces compete with Kubik on price. "Co-working is closely connected to the office market, and Budapest has a huge amount of office space, a lot of it empty," she said. According to consultancy Cushman and Wakefield, 12% of the city's office stock is vacant.

At Create26, events are a core strategy for member retention. The space regularly hosts the city's Claude AI Community meetups and maintains a close partnership with the non-profit Startup Hungary, frequently featuring workshops on building and scaling with AI or navigating investor funding. "It's a full package," said Create26's general manager Seloua Benkaid Kasbah. "So, you are here not only working alone at your desk, but you also find yourself at lunchtime talking with members from big companies ... and in the evening you find yourself at an event with founders and investors," she said.

Mocsár said that while remote work is "here to stay," she's not convinced a life of constantly moving around is sustainable. "What I see more is that people create a few fixed points for themselves around the world. Places they come back to from time to time, where there are familiar faces, and where they feel a little bit at home. And I think co-working spaces can play a really important role in that," she said.

While Budapest is undoubtedly popular with digital nomads, it remains an emerging market for flexible office space, accounting for just 1.7% to 1.8% of the city's total office stock, according to data from Cushman and Wakefield.

It ranked Hungary 31st on a list of 40 countries in its Global Trends in Flexible Office 2026 report — behind Spain and Portugal but ahead of Italy and Canada — while the Coworking Hungary Association views flexible solutions as "one of the most significant opportunities in the upcoming years."

For those looking to explore Hungary as a digital nomad, Vindisch's advises approaching the experience as a relocation rather than a visit, emphasizing the importance of sorting admin like banking and residency paperwork early. But, "most importantly," she said: "With a culture that's really uniquely beautiful, like Hungary, I would say come ready to embrace it and assimilate into it."

#2793. DOT Sec. Duffy ties router in southern Arkansas to issues that led to nationwide ground stop

Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy said Sunday that a router installed in southern Arkansas as part of an upgrade to a system overseen by L3Harris Technologies could be key to problems that led officials to order the biggest halt to flight departures to U.S. airports since 9/11 on Friday.

"We think it was the installation or the router that was the bad actor," Duffy said in an interview with Fox News. "We're open if we see something else, we're going to continue to investigate with our federal partners and law enforcement."

Duffy told ABC's "This Week" in a separate interview that the router was located in El Dorado in southern Arkansas. The equipment, along with another router that was in Amarillo, Texas, is at an L3Harris lab in Florida for examination, Duffy said.

He said the router, part of ongoing work to switch from copper wire to a fiber-optic system, began flooding other systems. Federal officials said on Saturday that "instability" in communication feeds and data like radar prompted the more than four-hour ground stop.

Flight disruptions lingered on Sunday but were far from the thousands of cancellations passengers faced on Friday and Saturday.

There was "zero evidence" that the problem was caused by something "nefarious" or anything related to artificial intelligence, Bryan Bedford, the head of the Federal Aviation Administration, said at a press conference with Duffy Saturday morning.

Bedford said no FAA facilities went dark but that officials decided to ground flights because of "instability" in information feeds to air traffic control centers that handle en route aircraft at high altitudes and eventually to others closer to airports.

The issue occurred during "routine" upgrades of switching from a copper wire system to fiber, Bedford said.

A router had "flooded" other routers in the air traffic control system with over 1,000 connection attempts roughly every five seconds, said Duffy. That created problems with airplane communications and radar feeds, they said.

"We were unwilling to take any risk that this propagation could actually result in a center losing connectivity with aircraft," said Bedford. He said officials weren't sure for about 45 minutes what was causing the instability.

The outage again thrust aging infrastructure that underpins U.S. air traffic control into the spotlight, along with the country's struggles to replace sections of it while operating the world's busiest airspace.

"What we're working to determine was whether we had incompetence, meaning was it user error, installator error from L3 Harris, or did they install a faulty router," he said.

L3Harris said it is "supporting the FAA in its investigation to understand the conditions that led to the Nov. 13 ground stop of our nation's air travel."

The company, a longtime U.S. contractor, said it has completed more than 70% of the switch from copper wiring to fiber-optic system.

Duffy took the groundstop to seek billions more from lawmakers to modernize the system.

Flight disruptions began to ease Sunday, though more than 2,000 flights were canceled. That was down from the roughly 6,500 cancellations Saturday and over 8,000 delays, according to flight-tracking site FlightAware. That's on top of more than 9,000 U.S. flight cancellations, about a third of the day's schedule, and more than 7,000 delays on Friday, though some disruptions were due to bad weather.

Friday had the second-highest U.S. flight cancellations since a severe blizzard and below-freezing temperatures in January, according to aviation-data firm Cirium.

Part of untangling from the ground stop included getting aircraft where they need to be. The ground stop forced many planes to divert to other airports, while dozens of planes had to park with limited gate space because flights couldn't depart.

Airlines run one of the lightest schedules on Saturday and are deploying as many spare planes and crews as possible to get travelers and employees where they need to be as they're eager to start fresh for the upcoming Thanksgiving holiday period.

American Airlines, for example, offered flight attendants extra pay to pick up trips.

Delta Air Lines offered crews double pay to pick up trips and gave them confirmed seats on flights to get them into position, according to internal company messages CNBC reviewed. United Airlines was also offering pilots and flight attendants extra pay to pick up flights through Sunday, according to people familiar with the matter.

American, along with other airlines, said it would change customers' tickets at no fee or fare difference if the outage affected their plans and that it would cap some airfares.

Around 3 million people were scheduled to fly on Saturday, according to Cirium.

The FAA had halted departing U.S. flights around 4 p.m. ET on Friday, a ground stop that lasted more than four hours, the longest such pause on departing flights in 25 years. A few flights began to depart even before the ground stop was officially lifted; however, like trans-oceanic flights that were quickly handed off to other air traffic facilities, airlines had to seek approval for them one by one.

Duffy on Friday blamed outdated U.S. infrastructure for the problem and said Congress needs to approve more money to overhaul the country's air traffic control. Aging equipment and a shortage of air traffic controllers have angered airline executives for years, who have complained about the cost of resulting disruptions.

It was the second time the FAA has issued a nationwide ground stop since the Sept. 11, 2001, terror attacks. Trump administration officials have repeatedly said it did not appear to be a cyberattack.

The U.S. has the world's busiest airspace.

"Before clearing flights to resume, we thoroughly tested our systems to confirm they were stable and that operations remain reliable," the FAA said late Friday.

The disruption also upended travel for NBA, NHL and college teams, which struggled to reach their destinations in time for games this weekend.

Duffy said Friday that college football teams traveling on charter flights could receive priority.

"I would encourage those football teams to reach out to the FAA to schedule their flight times and make sure they have games going off tomorrow," he said at the Friday night briefing.

The Southeastern Conference announced on X that some games had been moved to Sunday due to the flight disruption.

"We are in close communication with our member institutions to assess potential disruptions and coordinate appropriate responses," the SEC said in a statement shared on X.

The National Football League said that it is putting in three backup referees for three games on Sunday because of the ground stop, "reassigning officials to games within driving distance of their hometowns."

— CNBC's April Roach and Jessica Golden contributed to this article.

Down 17-0 in the first half versus the rival Cincinnati Bengals on Sunday Night Football, the Steelers' rough night continued with starting left guard Mason McCormick being carted off the field.

With 1:27 remaining in the half, McCormick was seen lying on the field with his right ankle at an uncomfortable angle.

His leg was placed in an air cast according to the Pittsburgh Post-Gazette's Ray Fittipaldo, and McCormick was carted off the field. The play was a fourth down, with Steelers kicker Chris Boswell bringing the score to 17-3 on a 45-yard field goal on the following play.
