#2791. Top Wall Street analysts are bullish on these 3 stocks for the long term

Major indexes were volatile this week due to earnings from artificial intelligence companies, key economic releases, and the scandal involving Nvidia former CEO Jensen Huang. While short-term fluctuations could weigh on investor sentiment, it is prudent to maintain a long-term investment horizon.

Investors looking for attractive long-term picks can track the ratings of top Wall Street analysts and gain key insights from their in-depth research on the macro and micro factors impacting stocks.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Cybersecurity company Palo Alto Networks will announce its fiscal first-quarter earnings on Nov. 18. Migration to the cloud and the rapid adoption of AI have increased demand for cybersecurity solutions provided by Palo Alto and its peers. TipRanks' KPIs tool for PANW shows the rise in the company's subscription revenue over the years, driven by the adoption of its AI and security offerings and contributions from recent acquisitions.

Ahead of the upcoming earnings, Oppenheimer analyst Ittai Kidron reiterated a buy rating on Palo Alto stock with a price target of $450. The five-star analyst said that his checks indicate upside potential from CyberArk and Chronosphere acquisitions and strength in the company's core businesses.

Specifically, Kidron expects Palo Alto's Q1 FY27 next-generation security annualized recurring revenue to be about two percentage points above the midpoint of the guidance range and consensus, or $9.55 billion. He also sees nearly 1 to 1.5 percentage points of revenue upside compared to the guidance and consensus of $3.305 billion. Kidron expects management to remain conservative with its FY27 guidance and raise it by no more than the size of the quarterly beat.

Additionally, Kidron highlighted that partner checks indicated above-plan performance in the quarter, driven by firewall-attached subscriptions and strength in Prisma SASE and XSIAM offerings. The analyst also expects results to gain from CyberArk cross-selling and better-than-expected performance of Chronosphere as OpenAI completes its migration.

"While Prisma AIRS crossed $100M in ARR last quarter, the fastest product ramp in company history, partners still view CY27 as the first real revenue year," said Kidron.

Kidron ranks No. 638 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 55% of the time, delivering an average return of 10.5%. See Palo Alto Technical Analysis on TipRanks.

Next on this week's list is database software company MongoDB. Recently, BTIG analyst Gray Powell initiated coverage of MDB stock with a buy rating and a price target of $417. Powell estimates that currently the size of the company's core addressable market is nearly $15 billion, with several tailwinds to drive more than 20% revenue growth over the next few years.

Beyond its core document and vector businesses, Powell highlighted that MongoDB has exposure to about $72 billion of adjacent markets, projected to grow to about $121 billion over four years. With even modest share gains in adjacent markets, the five-star analyst believes that MongoDB can grow revenue at about a 24% annual rate over the next three years, above management's growth target of more than 20%.

Contrary to the bearish narrative, Powell views AI as an emerging tailwind for MongoDB, with application modernization creating new workloads while making migrations faster and cheaper. He added that interviews with eight contacts were generally constructive and indicated faster legacy migrations, growing non-relational adoption, and early traction in Voyage AI and Vector Search offerings.

Additionally, Powell sees upside to Atlas growth, driven by new products. "We see a meaningful product cycle led by MongoDB 9.0 and AMP, with Agent Engine and Atlas Infinite adding upside over time," said the analyst.

Powell ranks No. 480 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 13%. See MongoDB Technical Analysis on TipRanks.

Membership-only warehouse chain Costco Wholesale recently announced a special cash dividend of $30 per share, payable on Dec. 4. The company also announced that its board has reauthorized a stock repurchase program of up to $4 billion, which will expire in November 2030 and replaces the current $4 billion program that is set to end in January 2027.

Following the announcement, Oppenheimer analyst Rupesh Parikh reiterated a buy rating on COST stock with a price target of $1,160. The five-star analyst said that the special dividend news is one of the key catalysts that he has been waiting for since Costco was added to Oppenheimer's top pick list in November 2025.

Parikh highlighted that the special dividend the company announced is significantly above his estimate of about $20 per share. The analyst expects the special dividend to reduce interest income substantially.

"COST remains a top pick for us, and we still see the potential for a stock split down the road, which could represent another positive catalyst for shares, in our view," said Parikh.

Overall, Parikh remains bullish on Costco's long-term prospects, backed by its value proposition, leading competitive position, and a solid track record of shareholder returns. The analyst also noted the company's strong management team and the ability to sustain top- and bottom-line growth even in a competitive retail backdrop.

Parikh ranks No. 324 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 12.7%. See Costco Statistics on TipRanks.

#2790. Tanks, Airport Tugs, and Golf Carts: Inside the Unhinged Facebook Marketplace Obsessions of NASCAR’s Biggest Stars

A scroll through Facebook Marketplace can begin with something ordinary: a used piece of furniture, a spare set of wheels or a car that looks too good to ignore. For some NASCAR drivers, however, browsing takes a considerably stranger turn.

Their wish lists stretch from airport baggage tugs and engine-swapped golf carts to old Toyotas, aircraft and, in one particularly extreme case, a military tank.

A Motorsport clip on X brought together Cup Series drivers to discuss their purchases and projects. Facebook Marketplace connects several of the most entertaining stories, although not every item mentioned was bought through the platform.

Chase Briscoe checks the listings, though he says he has not bought much there. x

"I get on Facebook Marketplace. I haven't bought a whole lot from there, but I get on there and just scroll it," he said. Instead, he sends interesting finds to Carson Hocevar, hoping his fellow driver will take the plunge so Briscoe can "vicariously live through him."

Hocevar mentioned wanting to get an old Spirit Airlines airplane. Briscoe, meanwhile, recalled seeing Oakland Airport selling its baggage tugs for very little money and sending the listing to Hocevar. The bargain was apparently enough to make them tempting. Briscoe may do the scrolling, but Hocevar is the one he trusts to act on an unusual listing.

Ryan Preece has a different Marketplace project in mind.

"I was actually looking at golf carts that were around $1,000," he said.

Preece explained that he has a 750cc motorcycle engine he had planned to put in a TQ midget for indoor racing.

Now, he is considering putting it in a golf cart instead. He wants not only the loudest truck in the D.O. lot, but also "the loudest golf cart and fastest." Quiet utility is clearly not the goal.

For someone who just won his third championship, one would expect Kyle Larson to be big on impulse purchases. Instead, he knows better than to get too involved. He said he is not a Facebook Marketplace user, and staying away is probably a good thing because he would likely buy plenty of things he does not need. That is sensible in this company.

The buying habits elsewhere in the group are just as varied. Daniel Suarez revealed that he had bought a Volkswagen Beetle the previous week, despite not planning to purchase one.

"I wasn't planning on buying it, but it was so nice," he explained.

Connor Zilisch has a more specific reward in mind: he keeps telling himself that a race win will earn him a Porsche 911 GT3 RS. Tyler Reddick offered a short answer about his interests: old Toyotas.

Denny Hamlin was less interested in naming a particular item than acknowledging his track record.

"I've made tons of bad purchases in my life. I just add another one to it," he joked.

Chris Buescher's interests occupy considerably more space. He described having a yard full of 1973–79 Dentside Ford F-100s and Broncos.

"My dad jokes that I basically have a wheel and tire shop," Buescher said.

Bubba Wallace's spending is more compact, but no less enthusiastic. He mentioned buying camera equipment, including a Leica M11-P Safari and an MP film camera, and admitted that he had bought a new camera he did not really need. The Safari's green finish was simply too appealing.

"My wife would be really happy if I didn't spend any money," he added.

Ty Gibbs approached the question from a practical angle. Rather than naming another car or recreational toy, he said a plane might be the best next purchase for his career.

"Time is money, and money is time. At the end of the day, being able to go places is important," he explained. It is a different calculation, but it shows how personal these drivers' priorities can be.

Hocevar's own most conspicuous purchase, however, sits in a category of its own. He bought a decommissioned British Chieftain tank from YouTuber WhistlinDiesel in September 2026.

In the Motorsport clip, Hocevar recalled that when the tank arrived.

"I had a lot of people come over and it was a great way to meet my neighbors."

The delivery became an unusual neighbourhood introduction.

The tank soon became more than a conversation piece. As reported by Heavy, Hocevar was taking part in an $800 beater-car challenge with his friend Vernon Hale when his Chevrolet Cobalt suffered mechanical problems. Rather than spend more than the car's purchase price trying to repair it, Hocevar used the newly arrived tank to flatten the disabled Chevy.

The video was titled "HOA is Upset With my New Tank!", although Heavy noted that no formal association notice or legal action had been publicly confirmed.

From Briscoe's Marketplace finds and Preece's golf-cart experiment to Buescher's collection and Hocevar's tank, the drivers' interests are anything but uniform. Some hunt for bargains, some build around machinery they own, and others try to resist temptation. Their off-track pursuits can be almost as entertaining as race day.

#2789. Nvidia board urged to consider 'potential return of Jensen Huang' by two major investors

Two major Nvidia shareholders are calling on the board of directors to weigh all options for the company's future leadership, including "the potential return of Jensen Huang."

Altimeter Capital CEO Brad Gerstner posted a letter on X on Friday that urges Nvidia's board to "pursue a slow, measured, and deliberate process regarding the future leadership of Nvidia." The letter is also signed by Atreides Management's Gavin Baker.

Nvidia was Altimeter Capital's largest disclosed equity holding in its most recent SEC filing, with a 9.4 million-share stake worth nearly $1.9 billion as of June 30. Atreides held 1.2 million shares worth nearly $250 million at the end of June.

The letter said Nvidia will be "capably led" by interim CEO Colette Kress, who served as Nvidia's CFO for 13 years before the board announced Thursday that she'd lead the company in Huang's absence.

"But the Founder and long time incredible leader of this extraordinary company has earned the right for a deliberate process that avoids any needless rush to replace him before all the facts are known," the letter concludes. The document did not call for Huang's immediate reinstatement.

Nvidia founder Jensen Huang resigned on Thursday amid multiple affair allegations, including one with suspected Chinese spy Alice Huang, who also worked with Nvidia customer Megaspeed. The Singapore-based company is being investigated by the Justice Department for possibly redirecting some of its supply of Nvidia's restricted artificial intelligence chips to China.

SpaceX CEO Elon Musk said on X Friday that he signed a petition asking to reinstate Huang. A petition circulating online called "Stand with Jensen" claims to have more than 100,000 signatures, including more than 5,000 current Nvidia employees.

Musk said on X Saturday that without Huang, some "critical deals will be lost."

Another letter sent Friday called on U.S. Secretary of Commerce Howard Lutnick to testify about Megaspeed before the House Intelligence Committee.

Lutnick has agreed to testify before the Senate Banking Committee on Dec. 10 about whether the Commerce Department is "properly performing its broader role of preventing China from acquiring advanced AI chips," according to the letter. Huang has also been asked to testify.

The Commerce Department originally launched an investigation into Megaspeed that was moved to the Justice Department in 2025 after Nvidia's lawyer contacted a senior Commerce official, according to The New York Times.

President Donald Trump is scheduled to visit China on Thursday, but doesn't plan to raise Nvidia and the suspected spy affair during his meeting with Chinese president Xi Jinping, according to U.S. officials. Musk is expected to join Trump on the trip to Shenzhen, China, for the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation summit on Nov. 18 and 19.

#2788. Top Wall Street analysts like these 3 dividend stocks for consistent income

Democrats' midterm election win has put the artificial intelligence trade in question, as voters clearly expressed opposition to the rapid buildout of data centers. The stock market could remain volatile amid AI trade concerns, macro uncertainties, and geopolitical tensions.

In this scenario, investors can add dividend-paying stocks to their portfolios to earn steady income. However, the extensive universe of dividend stocks can make selection challenging. Top Wall Street analysts' recommendations can help investors shortlist attractive dividend stocks, as these experts assign ratings after in-depth financial analysis.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Viper Energy (VNOM) is a subsidiary of Diamondback and owns and acquires mineral and royalty interests, primarily in the Permian Basin. The company reported upbeat third-quarter earnings, supported by recent acquisitions and continued development activity from Diamondback and third-party operators. Viper announced a base cash dividend of 50 cents per share for Q3 2026, payable on Nov. 19. Based on the base and variable dividends announced over the past 12 months, VNOM's dividend yield stands at 5.7%.

Following the Q3 results, TD Cowen analyst Aaron Bilkoski reiterated a buy rating on Viper Energy stock with a price target of $61. The analyst noted that the company delivered solid Q3 results, with production, including oil and natural gas liquids, exceeding both TD Cowen and consensus estimates.

The 5-star analyst highlighted that the key update was the announcement of two royalty acquisitions for $809 million in cash, including deferred consideration, and $82 million of newly issued shares.

Given the recently announced base dividend hike, Bilkoski expects Viper to direct incremental free cash flow toward buybacks. While the acquisitions increase leverage, the analyst believes that Viper has enough capacity for opportunistic buybacks.

Bilkoski noted that Viper has historically delivered one of the highest production per share growth profiles in his royalty coverage, but still trades at a notable discount to the royalty peer average and the company's historical FCF yield range. "In our view, its above-average growth warrants a higher valuation," said the analyst.

Bilkoski ranks No. 525 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 12.80%. See Viper Energy Stock Buybacks on TipRanks.

Exxon Mobil (XOM) reported robust Q3 2026 profits, as its U.S. refineries boosted production to capitalize on the high diesel fuel prices sparked by the conflict in the Middle East. The oil and gas giant announced a quarterly dividend of $1.07 per share, payable on Dec. 10, reflecting a 3.9% increase. This marks Exxon's 44th consecutive year of dividend increases. At an annualized dividend of $4.28 per share, XOM offers a yield of 2.53%.

Impressed by the Q3 print, TD Cowen analyst Jason Gabelman reiterated a buy rating on Exxon stock and raised his price target to $193 from $180 to reflect higher downstream earnings, with the expectation of increased mid-cycle margins.

Gabelman noted that the strength in Exxon's refining business continues to offset Middle East-related headwinds. He added that the company increased its ownership in LNG projects, which should reduce its Middle East concentration over time from 75% to 50% and lower the likelihood of diversification-focused mergers and acquisitions. Beyond LNG, Gabelman expects Exxon's growth in the 2030s to come from Guyana, Kashagan, Permian, and possibly Venezuela.

Gabelman noted that Exxon announced a dividend hike of nearly 4% and plans to maintain buybacks at $20 billion for 2027. "The decision to maintain reduces the pro-cyclicality nature of the buyback," said the analyst.

Gabelman ranks No. 347 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 66% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.90%. See Exxon Mobil Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Williams (WMB), which owns and operates energy infrastructure to deliver natural gas, reported strong third-quarter results. The company announced a quarterly dividend of $0.525 per share, or $2.10 per share annualized, payable on Dec. 28. WMB offers a dividend yield of 3.09%.

In reaction to the Q3 2026 results, CIBC analyst Robert Catellier reaffirmed a buy rating on Williams stock with a price target of $82. The analyst noted that Q3 results were essentially in line with expectations and reflected continued strength in execution across WMB's infrastructure portfolio.

Catellier said that Williams' Q3 performance was driven by higher transmission, storage, and power generation revenues, as well as contributions from the Momentum Midstream acquisition.

He noted that Williams maintained its full-year EBITDA guidance of $8.3 billion to $8.5 billion. In fact, results are tracking toward the higher end of the guidance range, backed by contributions from recently completed projects and the Momentum Midstream deal. WMB continues to target long-term EBITDA compound annual growth rate of at least 11% through 2030.

Catellier expects WMB's long-term targets to be supported by incremental transmission and storage opportunities, including Transco's Southeast Supply Enhancement II project and an 84 MMcf/d expansion of the Garden Connector project.

Catellier ranks No. 667 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 66% of the time, delivering an average return of 9.60%. See Williams Technical Analysis on TipRanks.

On Sunday, America's Team wasn't even Dallas's Team. 49ers fans showed up in big numbers for the game against the Cowboys at AT&T Stadium.

#2783. Social Security and the blue wave: What could happen to payroll taxes and senior tax breaks

Congress has six years to enact changes to Social Security to prevent cuts to retirement benefits. Senators elected in the Nov. 3 midterms will be in office when the deadline arrives, and their decisions may be critical in making tax changes that can affect Social Security.

During the midterm campaign season, tax policy surfaced in the debate over Social Security in two different ways. Many Democrats, and some Republicans, called for high earners to pay more in Social Security payroll taxes, while many Republicans touted a recently enacted tax deduction for adults 65 and older.

The diverging tactics come at a crucial moment for reform: The first could increase revenue flowing into the program. The second reduces eligible older adults' income taxes and can lower the taxes some beneficiaries pay on Social Security benefits, reducing program revenue.

Social Security trustees' latest projections show the trust fund used to pay retirement benefits may start to become depleted in 2032, when 78% of benefits would be payable.

Lawmakers do have some leeway — if the program's retirement and disability trust funds are combined, depletion would be pushed out to 2034, when 83% of scheduled benefits would be payable. But that's still a haircut for retirees — and it's coming soon.

"We've waited too long already, and the consequences are that the gap has grown larger," Shai Akabas, vice president of economic policy at the Bipartisan Policy Center, said of Social Security's shortfall.

On the campaign trail, midterm candidates largely avoided specifics on how they would reform Social Security. However, raising the payroll tax cap, also known as the taxable maximum, was a popular choice among Democratic candidates. That would require people who earn more than the cap, currently $184,500, to begin contributing payroll taxes for their earnings above that amount.

Meanwhile, some Republican candidates said they would extend the temporary senior tax deduction of up to $6,000 per eligible individual that is set to expire in 2028.

The deduction, part of the One Big Beautiful Bill Act passed in July of last year and billed as a senior bonus, was among the factors that moved Social Security trustees' retirement trust fund depletion projections three months sooner, according to an annual report released in June.

Here's what experts say could be in store now that Democrats have won control of the House and Senate.

Social Security is a pay-as-you-go system that relies on payroll taxes from workers to help make monthly benefit payments to millions of Americans.

In 2026, earnings up to $184,500 are subject to that tax. Workers and employers each contribute 6.2% of pay up to that threshold, while self-employed individuals pay 12.4%.

The wage cap will rise in 2027 to $192,300. It adjusts higher annually to reflect incremental changes in the average wage index.

On the campaign trail, some House and Senate candidates said the cap should be raised or eliminated entirely so high earners contribute more.

Following the election, Sen. Bernie Sanders, I-Vt., who is expected to become the next chair of the Social Security subcommittee, said on X on Nov. 7 that his proposal to increase benefits by at least $2,400 per year and "scrap the cap" on earnings subject to Social Security taxes would be a "top priority."

Outgoing Sen. Bill Cassidy, R-La., responded on X that the plan would "put a 12.4% tax" on people who are not millionaires or billionaires.

The idea of higher payroll taxes has broader support. A national survey fielded in February found most respondents — 79% — support raising taxes for wages over $400,000, according to the University of Maryland School of Public Policy. The change was the most popular option tested among the 2,509 adults surveyed.

Rising income inequality has meant the payroll tax cap no longer covers 90% of earnings, though that percentage was expected to remain constant when lawmakers enacted the last major reforms to the program in 1983, according to research from the Roosevelt Institute, a New York-based liberal think tank. The share of earnings covered by Social Security payroll taxes fell to around 83% in 2000 and has mostly stabilized at that level, according to the institute.

Despite candidates mentioning plans to change the cap during election season, some experts say Congress may put off tax hikes that could be politically unpopular.

"I think lawmakers may put some of these taxes on the table closer to 2032, when the Social Security trust fund hits insolvency," said Jessica Riedl, a fellow at the Urban-Brookings Tax Policy Center, which provides independent analyses of tax issues. "In Washington, they love to wait until the last minute."

If the payroll tax cap were lifted from just under $200,000 to $300,000 in earnings per year, that would cost someone at that higher income threshold about $6,700 more in direct payroll taxes, Riedl said.

"There's no interest on either side for these sorts of tax increases in the short term," Riedl said. In the long term, though, "everything is on the table," she said.

While many Democrats have pushed for raising those taxes, some Republicans have also shared their openness to doing so.

"There's a crack in the dam there breaking," Dan Adcock, director of government relations and policy at the National Committee to Preserve Social Security and Medicare, said of the Republican support.

Even if lawmakers raise or eliminate the cap, they would still need to agree on other changes to shore up the program. Republicans may want benefit cuts such as raising the retirement age in exchange for increasing taxes, Adcock said.

Just how far adjusting the taxable maximum would go to help solve Social Security's funding woes depends on how it is applied.

If the cap is eliminated starting in 2027, with the full 12.4% tax applying to all earnings and no benefit credit for earnings above the current threshold, that would eliminate 58% of the long-range actuarial balance over 75 years, according to a September analysis by the Social Security Administration.

Applying the 12.4% payroll tax rate to earnings over $250,000 starting in 2027 could address 57% of the long-range balance, according to the SSA, provided there are no benefit increases in exchange for the extra contributions.

If the 12.4% payroll tax rate were applied to earnings over $400,000 starting in 2027 without benefit increases, that would eliminate 53% of the long-range actuarial balance, the agency's analysis found.

Eventually, all earnings would be subject to Social Security payroll taxes as the gap between the taxable maximum, which increases every year, and the new threshold closes over time.

Yet some experts say raising the payroll tax cap could have consequences for taxpayers and the economy.

Raising the cap would be an "unprecedented increase" in marginal tax rates, according to William McBride, chief economist at the Tax Foundation, an independent tax policy research organization.

Lifting the threshold could add more than 12 percentage points to the cost of earning an additional dollar of wages, McBride said. Those higher costs could affect the labor market, which may see higher unemployment as a result of the change, he said.

OBBBA's new senior tax deduction of up to $6,000 per individual — or "bonus additional amount for seniors," as it was dubbed in an early version of the legislation — is in effect for tax years 2025 through 2028.

Lawmakers elected to Congress in the midterms may decide whether to extend the temporary tax break.

"2028 is going to be a presidential election year, and I would not expect either party to be willing to allow any senior tax cuts to expire while they're competing for senior votes," Riedl said.

When President Donald Trump signed OBBBA into law in 2025, the White House said in a release: "No tax on Social Security is a reality." However, the new law does not eliminate taxes on Social Security benefits.

Instead, it provides a deduction that reduces seniors' taxable income regardless of whether they have claimed Social Security benefits.

During the campaign, Republicans touted the $6,000 senior deduction as tax relief and criticized Democrats for voting against the law that enacted it. However, congressional Democrats unanimously opposed OBBBA, citing provisions including cuts to Medicaid and food assistance.

Some effects of the new $6,000 senior deduction are different from those of eliminating taxes on benefits, according to McBride at the Tax Foundation. The senior deduction affects lower- and middle-income taxpayers to a greater degree, while eliminating taxes on Social Security benefits would be more beneficial to upper-income taxpayers, McBride said.

How Social Security benefits are currently taxed

Individuals with $25,000 to $34,000 in combined income — the sum of adjusted gross income, tax-exempt interest and half of annual Social Security benefits — pay federal taxes on up to 50% of their Social Security benefits. For individuals with over $34,000 in combined income, up to 85% of their Social Security benefits are subject to federal income tax.

For married couples filing jointly with between $32,000 and $44,000 in combined income, up to 50% of their Social Security benefits are subject to federal income tax. For married couples with more than $44,000 in combined income, that increases to up to 85% of their benefits.

The senior bonus tax deduction allows those age 65 and older to deduct up to $6,000 for individuals, or up to $12,000 for married couples. The deduction phases out for modified adjusted gross income over $75,000 for single filers and $150,000 for married couples.

The senior bonus deduction — together with the tax year 2025 standard deduction of $15,750 for single taxpayers and $31,500 for married couples and the existing senior deduction for those who qualify of $2,000 for singles and $3,200 for married couples — "amounts to the largest tax break in history for America's seniors," the White House said in July 2025. In 2026, the standard deduction is $16,100 for single filers and $32,200 for married couples.

Under OBBBA, 88% of beneficiaries, or 51.4 million individuals age 65 and older receiving Social Security income, will have exemptions and deductions that exceed their taxable Social Security income, according to the administration. Prior to the law, 64% of beneficiaries, or 37.2 million individuals, had tax breaks that exceeded their taxable Social Security income.

But the measure does nothing to resolve Social Security's depletion problem.

In an annual report released in June, Social Security's trustees cited OBBBA as having a "substantial effect" on the program's financial status because beneficiaries will pay less in income taxes on benefits. In that report, the trustees moved the projected depletion date for the program's retirement trust fund to the fourth quarter of 2032 — up one quarter from the previous year's projections.

"This new deduction for seniors cuts into some of the funding for the trust fund, so it makes the solvency date a little nearer in time," McBride said. "It makes the program a little more fragile, if you will."

#2781. Tokenization platform Securitize gains 10% postmarket on Q3 results, move into asset-backed finance

Tokenization platform Securitize climbed about 10% postmarket after reporting third quarter growth alongside increased operating expenses, and announcing an expansion into asset-backed finance through a strategic acquisition.

Miami-based Securitize, which made its public market debut in July, operates in the relatively new corner of the crypto equity universe involved in institutional tokenization infrastructure. Tokenized assets on the platform grew 9% in the third quarter, to $4.8 billion, from the same period a year ago. Aggregate transaction volume rose 20% from a year ago, to $4.3 billion. Revenue of $11.5 million was up 28% from last year.

The growth came as bullish sentiment around tokenization gained traction throughout the third quarter, ahead of the Security and Exchange Commission formalizing the long awaited Innovation Exemption on Sept. 17, which created a temporary pathway for trading of certain tokenized stocks and strengthened the regulatory outlook.

Profitability is still meaningfully below year-ago levels, due in part to costs associated with going public over the summer, as well as the scaling up of the tokenization business at an active time for the entire sector, Securitize CEO Carlos Domingo told CNBC in an exclusive interview.

Securitize reinforced "certain areas of the company like finance, compliance, legal [and] cybersecurity," which increased corporate costs "compared to a year ago when we were privately held," Domingo said. "We're in in a very good position. The adjusted EBITDA … is improving significantly from previous quarters."

Securitize reported an adjusted EBITDA loss of $2 million, compared with positive adjusted EBITDA of $3.6 million a year ago, better than a $5.5 million loss in the second quarter. The company posted a 183% increase in operating costs and expenses.

Also on Monday, the Securitize board authorized a $50 million share buyback and announced the acquisition of River One, an institutional asset-backed financing platform developing technology to connect stablecoin capital with traditional lending markets. Terms were not disclosed.

Securitize sees an opportunity to modernize the $9 trillion market for asset backed finance by using stablecoins to finance real world assets, generate yield and tokenize investment positions and credit products, Domingo said.The acquisition also marks an expansion beyond Securitize's core niche markets at a time when tokenization's competitive landscape is growing, with traditional asset managers like BlackRock and Franklin Templeton, and established financial market infrastructure providers like DTCC and Broadridge, entering the industry.

"We have been very active in tokenized funds, treasuries [and] yield … And we've been obviously recently very active in tokenized stocks, so this adds another piece of the puzzle in the emerging tokenization space for us to capture revenue growth."

#2779. Observations after Sixers beat Bucks to move to 3-0, Embiid posts triple-double

Observations after Sixers beat Bucks to move to 3-0, Embiid posts triple-double originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Sixers stayed unbeaten Saturday night at Xfinity Mobile Arena, earning a 127-116 win over the Bucks and improving to 3-0 on the season.

Joel Embiid had his 10th career regular-season triple-double, tallying 28 points, 11 rebounds and 10 assists.

On his 30th birthday, Jaylen Brown posted 27 points and five assists. Tyrese Maxey had 26 points. VJ Edgecombe scored 17 and grabbed 13 boards.

The Sixers' absences on the first night of a back-to-back included LeBron James (low back/sciatica management). Sixers head coach Nick Nurse said pregame that he did not know yet who would be available for his team's matchup with the Pistons on Sunday.

Here are observations on the Sixers' victory Saturday:

Dean Wade made his Sixers debut and started in James' place. He'd missed the first two games of the regular season with a left ankle sprain.

The 3-and-D forward scored the night's first points by a sinking a corner three-pointer on the opening possession.

Embiid also had a sharp start. He moved well, made good decisions and served as the main hub of his team's offense in the early going.

The Sixers' seven-time All-Star center has often enjoyed facing Myles Turner. He entered the night with an average of 29.4 points in 18 previous games against the Bucks big man.

Embiid did a little bit of everything in the first quarter, posting eight points, four assists and four rebounds over nine minutes. After snatching an offensive board, Embiid drained a fadeaway jumper to give the Sixers a 10-1 lead.

One of Embiid's assists was quite stylish. He found Edgecombe open beyond the arc with a behind-the-back bounce pass. Edgecombe knocked down the shot and wound up going 3 for 4 from three-point range in the first quarter.

The Sixers added rookie Labaron Philon Jr. to their rotation. Jabari Walker stayed in the mix, though he didn't enter until the 7:36 mark of the second quarter.

Philon played four minutes in his NBA debut and missed his lone shot attempt, a corner three. On defense, Philon allowed a couple of blow-bys off the dribble. He generally looked like he belonged on offense, albeit without any production in his brief run.

Maxey banked in a half-court heave at the first-quarter buzzer to put the Sixers up nine points. The Sixers couldn't carry forward any momentum, however, and began the second quarter poorly.

Adem Bona had another rough stint amid a subpar start to the season. After Anfernee Simons missed a transition layup try, Ryan Rollins' three lifted the Bucks into a 40-37 lead.

The dreadful stretch for the Sixers on both sides of the ball called to mind how they'd fallen into a 29-point deficit Thursday night vs. the Cavaliers before clawing out a comeback win.

The Sixers mustered just seven points in first six minutes of the second quarter and had many stagnant possessions that concluded with contested jumpers. The team's transition defense was dismal for much of the second period. Milwaukee had the game's first 19 fast-break points.

The Sixers regained the lead late in the second quarter. Kentavious Caldwell-Pope made two three-balls in the second and nabbed three first-half steals. Embiid assisted a go-ahead, and-one Walker layup.

Brown hit a three with 11.8 seconds left in the second quarter. Unsurprisingly, his variety of shotmaking skills were useful without James in the lineup.

Even when James or Embiid sit, the Sixers should have a simple advantage in terms of volume of stars over most opponents this year. None of Brown, Embiid or Maxey had the sort of off night that would have improved the Bucks' odds of a road win. They'd all passed the 20-point mark by the end of the third quarter.

Maxey notched another final-possession bucket, this time with a more conventional make. He canned a jumper from the right elbow with 1.7 seconds left in the third to extend the Sixers' lead to 97-87.

The Bucks made a late push in the fourth quarter. Kel'el Ware's put-back, and-one layup cut the Milwaukee's deficit to 117-114.

The Sixers ultimately handled business. Embiid reached his triple-double by assisting a second-chance Brown jumper and and Caldwell-Pope soon drilled a dagger corner three. Embiid even threw down a last-minute dunk for good measure.

For the second consecutive season, the Sixers have a 3-0 start. They got to four wins last year before dropping their first game.

#2777. They train people to avoid 'honeypot' scams—here are the common red flags they spot: 'It feels so good to be wanted'

Shawnee Delaney often kicks off a training session by telling a room full of corporate workers: "OK, raise your hand if you've ever gotten a flattering message on LinkedIn just complimenting your background and your profile and your experience."

"Every hand goes up," she says. Then, she tells attendees how responding to a simple adulatory message could unwittingly open the door to a potential romance scam.

Delaney is a corporate security expert, well-versed in teaching people how to avoid “honeypot” schemes, in which a bad actor uses romantic or sexual interest to build trust and gain information, access or leverage over a target. The term is currently making headlines in the wake of former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang's resignation from the company, following reporting on his admitted affair with an alleged Chinese spy.

Honeypots, also known as "honey traps," are a very human form of social engineering attack, a category that also includes various forms of phishing and use of deepfake technology. And you don't have to be a billionaire tech CEO to possess information that scammers may value.

As competition in the global artificial intelligence race heats up, OpenAI is reportedly training employees to recognize espionage tactics including bribery, romantic manipulation and blackmail, according to The Wall Street Journal. On Nov. 3, cryptocurrency exchange Binance revealed that a recent cyberattack — which saw roughly $6 billion of digital assets stolen — was partially made possible by a fake job interview scheme that infected an employee’s work computer with malware.

"People can get solicited, bribed, romanced, etc.," says Kevin Tian, co-founder and CEO of Doppel, an AI-powered platform meant to help identify and deflect social engineering attacks. He adds: "If you've got access to sensitive IP [intellectual property] — it's not just AI companies — you can be a target."

Increasingly, scammers can use AI to scour websites ranging from Reddit to Pinterest for an individual's personal information and customize a ploy designed specifically for them, Tian says. Once they have relevant data points, attackers tend to use "the oldest tricks in the book: elicitation, flattery, ego," says Delaney, a former intelligence officer with the Defense Intelligence Agency and the founder and CEO of Washington, D.C.-based corporate security consulting firm Vaillance Group.

In many cases, Delaney notes, targets have an inkling that something is suspicious — but they'd rather allow themselves to believe the interest is legitimate. So how do you keep yourself from falling victim? Trust your self-awareness, common sense and ability to identify situations that feel too good to be true, she says.

"The bottom line is, we're all human," says Delaney, adding: "It feels so good to be wanted, or to be respected, or to be appreciated, or loved."

Delaney, who has worked with Fortune 500 companies such as Uber and Merck, advises corporate clients on cybersecurity risks and develops training programs to improve employees' awareness of security threats. Her sessions aim to build awareness of manipulative connections, such as romance scams, from how they begin to the natural progression they often take. "Introduction, then repeated contact, favors, sensitive requests — the secrecy, the pressure," she says.

Bad actors may tap into any information you post online to find an interest that can be mirrored and exploited, or an acquaintance who can be infiltrated and make an introduction. If someone you know and trust makes an introduction, you might be less likely to "go do due diligence" on the new person, even if they're a stranger, Delaney says.

One of Vaillance's services includes building a "targeting package" around an executive or C-Suite member, says Delaney. "We can show how they are vulnerable and how we would target that organization just by leveraging the people around them in that circle: spouses, friends, kids, parents, administrative assistants, neighbors," she says. "That's when people's eyes open."

From there, she discusses red flags to spot. Conversations that begin with flattery and seemingly innocuous small talk can gradually shift toward digging for information, introductions to colleagues or access to documents, says Delaney. It's human nature to want to be helpful and fulfill friendly requests, she adds. Add a potential romantic connection to the mix, especially amid an ongoing "loneliness epidemic," and the urge to comply can be even harder to resist, she says.

If you feel yourself getting cornered in a situation where a recent acquaintance or romantic partner keeps asking for information you're not comfortable revealing, Delaney says it helps to have first practiced polite refusal. "One of my favorites is: They could just respond with: 'Why do you ask?'" she says. "Put the other person on the defensive. You could give a nondescript answer. You can say you don't know. You can say you can't respond."

If you're ever suspicious of a new connection — even if you don't want to be — do yourself a favor and conduct some basic vetting, she recommends. You might examine their social media profiles and other publicly available information, for example, to see if their story checks out.

If you can't find enough information to allay your suspicions, don't be shy about alerting your employer's security or human resources team, she says. For smaller companies, or scams outside the workplace, you can file a complaint for cyber threats or romance scams with the Federal Bureau of Investigation's Internet Crime Complaint Center.

And pay attention if your new paramour starts asking you specific questions about your finances or work credentials, says Delaney.

"If you have no game at home, and you get off an airplane somewhere and suddenly everyone's all over you, we call that a red flag," she says.

#2775. James reaches assists milestone, keeps seeing plays ‘before they actually happen'

James reaches assists milestone, keeps seeing plays 'before they actually happen' originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

Tyrese Maxey admitted that he wasn't entirely ready for LeBron James' first pass of the afternoon Sunday.

Maxey cut through the paint and James' bounce pass was perfect, but the Sixers' All-NBA guard missed his layup. He's not fully accustomed yet to James' precognitive gifts.

"There's still some times when you're surprised," Maxey said after the Sixers' win over the Pacers. "… I was cutting through just to cut and create space. The ball hit me in the chest and I missed the layup. I was like, 'Ah shoot, my bad.' He was like, 'Yeah, you weren't looking.'"

James has been trying to teach his teammates that he'll spot openings they may not notice.

"I just tell my guys, 'Always pay attention to me when I've got the ball,' because I definitely see plays develop before they happen," James said.

For the most part, James' teammates adhered to his request on the latest historic day in a milestone-filled career. By setting up VJ Edgecombe's layup with 1:19 left in the fourth quarter, James moved into third place on the NBA's all-time assists list, passing Jason Kidd.

The 22-time All-Star recorded 11 assists, his most so far as a Sixer, and now sits at 12,092 in his career. Chris Paul (12,552 assists) and John Stockton (15,806) are the only two players above James.

"It's obviously super humbling," James said. "It's something that I didn't foresee in my career, but I knew assists meant a lot to me. Just the movement of the ball, being able to find my teammates, putting the ball on time and on target.

"And also just being linked with some of the greats that have ever played this game and a dear friend of mine, J-Kidd. It's really special."

James is at or near the top of many all-time leaderboards. He's first in NBA history in points, games and minutes, sixth in steals and fifth in triple-doubles.

Passing has always been a core element of his game.

"First of all, he's got the vision and the awareness," Sixers head coach Nick Nurse said. "He's got the size to see. I think he's got some anticipation. Just a half-second before a play happens, he knows how the defense is moving. … And he throws crisp passes. Some of those are cooking out to the corner three guys, so it doesn't let the close-out get there nearly as quickly."

Whether it's a long-range rocket, a drive-and-kick dish or a cross-court feed from the post that suggests he might have eyes in the back of his head, James has always enjoyed finding a wide range of ways to help his teammates.

"For someone that talented, he's very unselfish," Maxey said. "He really prides himself on getting guys open."

As James recalled Sunday, he had exceptional passing tools long before he entered the NBA 24 seasons ago.

"Since I was a kid, I've had the ability to see plays before they actually happen," he said. "Seeing movement, seeing cracks in the defense before they were even open … it's something that's been with me since I was a kid.

"I just had that vision and it just stuck with me throughout my playing days."

#2772. SpaceX catches upper stage of Starship rocket in historic first

After a booster explosion on Friday, SpaceX successfully caught the upper stage, or spacecraft, portion of its massive Starship rocket from orbit on Saturday — a triumphant milestone for the company.

SpaceX made the historic catch using its "chopsticks," mechanical arms attached to a launch tower, at its facility in Starbase, Texas.

Test Flight 15, the most ambitious Starship test yet, marked the first time Elon Musk's company attempted to catch both the rocket's booster and upper stage.

Musk posted a video on X inspecting the upper stage craft after completing the capture.

During the first phase of the test flight on Friday, SpaceX failed to catch and recover the booster, instead diverting it to an offshore splashdown in the Gulf of Mexico where it exploded before hitting the water.

The rocket's upper stage then continued into orbit and flew around the Earth 14 times before successfully landing on SpaceX's launch tower, nicknamed Mechazilla.

The Hawaii Department of Transportation issued a temporary pause on some West Coast flights Friday, due to debris risk from the flight. Separately, the FAA issued a rare, nationwide ground stop due to telecommunications system failures. (Both of those orders have since been lifted.)

Making Starship a reliable and fully reusable rocket is critical to SpaceX's ambitions. In the near term, the company needs Starship to send more satellites into orbit to expand its Starlink satellite internet service, which is currently the company's profit center.

SpaceX is also expected to use Starship to bring astronauts and cargo from lunar orbit to the Moon's surface and back for NASA. Musk also has a grand vision to use Starship to "colonize Mars" and "make humanity multi-planetary."

During Test Flight 15 on Friday, SpaceX released another 26 of its newest Starlink v3 satellites into orbit, growing its commercial satellite internet network.

The company's constellation already included over 11,000 satellites in low Earth orbit. One Starship rocket can carry up to 60 Starlink v3 satellites, the company has said.

#2771. Patrick Mahomes Sends Scary Message on Chiefs’ New-Look Offense

Wide receiver Josh Downs of the Kansas City Chiefs. Getty

The Chiefs deliver an update on quarterback Patrick Mahomes after the Jets game. Getty

Quarterback Patrick Mahomes had the Kansas City Chiefs humming against the Falcons in Atlanta, which he believes is proof of what the offense can be down the stretch of the season and into the playoffs.

Mahomes spoke candidly and optimistically about his team's monster afternoon on Sunday, November 15 during the postgame press conference.

"I think more than anything it just proves that we can attack down the field like we believe that we can," Mahomes said. "And defenses have done a job of kinda holding shell coverages and stuff like that, but if they're gonna come down and try to stop the run, we have to prove that we can throw it down the field. And it showcases another level to our offense that we can continue to get better and better."

GettyWide receiver Josh Downs of the Kansas City Chiefs.

Kansas City's offense hung 35 points on its opponent for the second consecutive week following a two-game losing streak, which are also the Chiefs' only two defeats of the season through Week 10.

Mahomes had an epic performance, completing 22-of-35 passes for 348 yards and five touchdowns. He was able to do so even despite tight end Travis Kelce suffering an ankle injury in the first quarter. And though Kelce later returned to the game, he finished the day with just two catches for 30 yards.

Meanwhile, starting running back Kenneth Walker III, a transformative star for Kansas City since joining the team via free agency during the offseason, sat out the contest after suffering a groin strain in practice on Thursday.

But even without Walker to provide one of the top rushing threats in the NFL, Mahomes was still able to spread the football around to a group of wide receivers that now includes Josh Downs following a trade deadline with the Indianapolis Colts last week.

Downs had four grabs for 53 yards and one score in his Chiefs debut, while Rashee Rice led the way with five catches for 82 yards. Backup running back/rookie Emmett Johnson got the start for Walker and caught six passes for 70 yards and two scores.

Meanwhile, fifth-round rookie Cyrus Allen tallied the first TD of his career, adding two grabs for 44 yards, while Andrew Armstrong also scored his first-ever touchdown on a 43-yard bomb.

GettyKansas City Chiefs quarterback Patrick Mahomes.

Kansas City's explosive play rate boomed on Sunday, as the Chiefs hit 10 such plays on the day. An explosive in the run game is defined as any rush of 10 or more yards, while explosives in the pass game are any completions of 20 or more yards.

Mahomes hit seven passes of at least 20-plus yards, while the run game produced three rushes of 10-plus yards. That means that roughly 16 percent of the Chiefs' 63 offensive plays in Atlanta were explosives, which will render any offense difficult to beat.

Kansas City (7-2) will look to keep the offense rolling against the Arizona Cardinals at Arrowhead Stadium on November 22 before what is unquestionably going to be the team's toughest stretch of schedule of the season.

The Chiefs play three consecutive road contests against the Buffalo Bills (8-1) on Thanksgiving Day, Los Angeles Rams (6-4) on December 3 and Cincinnati Bengals (6-2) on December 13. The team then returns to Kansas City for games against the New England Patriots (5-5) on December 21 and the San Francisco 49ers (8-1) on December 27.

#2770. What's ahead for AI financing after Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang departs

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang's departure hasn't produced a broad shock to the credit market, but it is putting the company's role as a financier of artificial intelligence infrastructure under scrutiny.

Huang, who stepped down Thursday amid an espionage and adultery scandal, built Nvidia into an AI powerhouse in the 33 years since he co-founded the company. Chief financial officer Colette Kress has been named interim CEO as the company searches for a permanent chief executive.

Nvidia not only supplies its graphics processing units to hyperscalers and tech giants, it also provides financing to other companies to help the industry expand. That could all change.

"Although we expect the company to fulfill all of its previously announced commitments, we believe that going forward, it may be more difficult for the company to negotiate new multi-party investments among ecosystem partners at the same scale it has done up to this point," Goldman Sachs analyst James Schneider said in a note Thursday.

The news of Huang's resignation comes after investors were already concerned about the massive amount of debt being taken on to fund AI infrastructure.

Tech bond yields have risen and spreads have widened on credit default swaps, most notably in lower-rated names. Credit default swaps are contracts in which investors pay for protection in case the borrower can't repay its debt. Wider spreads indicate investors view the bonds as riskier.

On Friday, the yield on Nvidia's 4.5% bond due in 2031 sat at 5.717%, nearly 5% higher week to date. Bond yields move inversely to prices.

Tech stocks slumped Friday with the Nasdaq 100 closing down 1.2% as markets continued to digest the Nvidia news. Yields on hyperscaler bonds ticked slightly higher on Friday, signaling the broader concern about AI debt lingers.

It's the smaller AI companies that could ultimately see an impact if Nvidia makes any changes to its AI financing. The company had $36 billion in off-balance-sheet AI cloud agreements, or backstops, for neoclouds, according to its last quarterly financial filing in July.

Neoclouds CoreWeave, Nebius and IREN are all in Nvidia's portfolio. Their shares were down sharply Thursday.

CoreWeave, which has become the poster child for AI debt fears, saw its bond yields retreat after its quarterly earnings on Tuesday eased investor concerns. The yield on its 9.75% bond due in 2031 was 14.79% on Friday after climbing to 16.7% last week. The spread on its five-year CDS also dropped more than 17% week to date, although it remained above 1,000 — which is generally considered a sign of distress.

It remains unclear how Nvidia's support for those companies may change, said CreditSights analyst Andy Li.

"We don't know what the ultimate strategy for Nvidia is going to be coming out the other side," he said.

"However, I think directionally, just given the fact that Jensen was so crucial in selling the Nvidia story and being a very aggressive deal maker, it's likely that a lot of this creative deal-making and circular financing and equity and credit support is going to moderate," he added.

CoreWeave CEO Michael Intrator told CNBC Wednesday, before Huang's departure, that Nvidia "is not one person."

"There are thousands of people over there that interface with thousands of people at my company every day. They integrate on the technology. They integrate on the physical infrastructure. They integrate on the networking," he said in an interview with "Squawk on the Street." "They have been a great partner for us for years. They will continue to be a great partner for us."

Bob Lang, founder and chief strategist at Explosive Options, doesn't see any big changes in terms of Nvidia's relevance or its ability to finance for now. As CFO, Kress was involved in the financing deals, and the search for a permanent CEO is going to take some time, he noted.

The real issue is just how much money the company will want to lend for AI financing, rather than, say using its hundreds of billions in free cash flow for other purposes, like share buybacks, capex, or acquisitions.

"Big companies like Nvidia and Meta, they're holding all the cards," he said. "When they decide that the game is over, that's it — it's going to be curtains for some of these smaller companies."

— CNBC'S Kif Leswing and Isabel O'Brien contributed reporting.

#2768. Lutnick faces questions over Nvidia spy scandal. His sons' firm has ties to the chipmaker

In late September, Cantor Fitzgerald hosted Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang and other top executives from the chipmaker for investor meetings in New York, according to a Cantor research report reviewed by CNBC.

The Wall Street firm liked what it heard.

"We continue to view NVDA as the best risk/reward in our coverage," Cantor analyst C.J. Muse and his colleagues wrote in a research report dated Oct. 3 that maintained a $350 price target and overweight rating on the stock.

Yet the firm, formerly run by Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick and now led by his sons, Brandon and Kyle, reported a nearly 50% decline in the number of Nvidia shares it held between September 2025 and March 2026, according to a CNBC analysis of securities filings that excludes options. Nvidia had been one of the largest stock positions reported in Cantor's filings.

The decline coincided with White House and U.S. intelligence officials pressing Lutnick to crack down on Megaspeed, a Singapore-based company suspected of funneling Nvidia's advanced artificial intelligence chips to China. Lutnick repeatedly resisted those efforts, Axios reported Nov. 11.

By the time Cantor hosted Huang in New York, Lutnick had also received a classified intelligence warning, according to a Nov. 10 Axios report, about Huang's ties to a suspected Chinese spy and had appeared alongside the CEO at a G20 technology summit in North Carolina.

Even as Cantor pared back its Nvidia stake, it continued doing business across the chipmaker's AI empire. A CNBC review of securities filings, corporate announcements and Wall Street research found the firm has helped arrange billions of dollars in financing for Nvidia's business partners while maintaining close ties to the chipmaker's leadership.

Those ties have drawn fresh scrutiny following Huang's resignation Thursday and Axios' reporting that Lutnick repeatedly resisted a crackdown on companies suspected of smuggling Nvidia chips to China, despite President Donald Trump authorizing new export restrictions on those companies for a third time on Nov. 5.

An ethics expert said the relationship between Lutnick and his former firm's investments may raise questions, given what the secretary knew about Nvidia.

Richard Painter, the former chief White House ethics lawyer for President George W. Bush and now a corporate law professor at the University of Minnesota, said that while "there's no proof of" Lutnick sharing nonpublic information with Cantor Fitzgerald, "the appearance doesn't look great."

"His conversations with his sons better be about publicly available information," Painter told CNBC.

There is no evidence that Lutnick's sons had advance knowledge of the intelligence concerns, that changes in Cantor's reported holdings were tied to any warnings or that the firm's business interests influenced their father's decisions.

"Cantor has to date from time to time had only immaterial stand-alone proprietary positions in Nvidia, unlike the positions reflected in 13F filings which were entered into in the ordinary course of business in connection with client swap transactions, that are common in institutional markets," a Cantor spokesperson told CNBC.

Cantor declined to answer CNBC's other questions. The Commerce Department, the White House, Howard Lutnick, Brandon Lutnick and Kyle Lutnick did not respond to CNBC's requests for comment or detailed questions about the findings.

As scrutiny of Huang intensified, Cantor Chairman Brandon Lutnick was publicly praising the chipmaker's leadership. "Nvidia is one of the great companies on the planet, and they have an amazing leadership team," Brandon Lutnick told Fox Business on Wednesday.

Trump is standing by Howard Lutnick. A White House official told Reuters on Thursday that the president maintains "complete confidence" in the commerce secretary.

But cracks in the administration's unity emerged Friday, when Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent told CNBC's "Squawk Box" that the departments of Treasury, Defense and State and the Office of the U.S. Trade Representative all "were in favor of putting Megaspeed on the entity list for months."

Bessent pointed to Commerce as the department responsible for imposing the entity list, which identifies restricted businesses and other groups, adding, "You will have to ask them about their internal processes."

Nvidia was Cantor's second-largest reported stock position before Howard Lutnick left Wall Street to become commerce secretary in February 2025, behind only bitcoin investor MicroStrategy, now known as Strategy. At the end of 2024, the firm disclosed more than 3.5 million Nvidia shares worth $479 million, according to its quarterly securities filing.

That share count held steady through September 2025, but its value climbed 36% to $652 million, according to Cantor's quarterly securities filing, as the Trump administration moved over the summer to ease restrictions on Nvidia's H20 chip sales to China.

Over the following six months, however, the number of Nvidia shares Cantor reported holding fell by 49%, to 3 million in December and 1.78 million by the end of March 2026, with their value dropping from $652 million to $310 million, according to a CNBC analysis of securities filings.

Pressure on Howard Lutnick was also mounting on Capitol Hill. Sens. Jim Banks, R-Ind., and Elizabeth Warren, D-Mass., in March urged the secretary to suspend or reconsider Nvidia's export licenses after three people linked to Nvidia customer Super Micro Computer were indicted on charges of conspiring to smuggle AI chips to China.

"The Trump administration, I think, leaks like a sieve when it comes to nonpublic government information getting to various hedge fund guys," Painter said, though he stressed there is no evidence Howard Lutnick had shared such information with his sons.

The filings do not establish why Cantor's reported share count fell or whether its overall financial exposure to Nvidia declined. Nvidia’s stock had more than doubled from its April 2025 lows by late October, before retreating in the following months. The stock fell about 6.5% between the end of September and the end of March.

Cantor's reported Nvidia holdings remained near that lower level through June 30, the latest quarter covered by public disclosures, at 1.81 million shares. Its next quarterly filing is due Monday.

Cantor has not publicly explained the decline.

Most of those shares were attributed to CF Secured, a Cantor brokerage subsidiary that uses stocks and derivatives to hedge customer transactions, according to its audited financial statements. Cantor also disclosed substantial Nvidia options positions during the time period CNBC examined, overwhelmingly skewed toward put options, which can protect against declines in the stock price.

As concerns about Huang escalated inside the Trump administration in May, according to the Nov. 11 Axios report, Cantor was helping finance the expansion of one of Nvidia's newest business partners, securities filings show.

On May 7, Nvidia and Australia-based cloud computing provider IREN announced a five-year, $3.4 billion cloud computing contract and a strategic partnership giving the chipmaker the right to invest up to $2.1 billion in IREN.

"That's not a passive financial investment. Nvidia is a partner who wins as we deliver," IREN co-CEO Daniel Roberts told investors on the company's quarterly earnings call that day.

Five days later, as Trump headed to China aboard Air Force One, Secretary of State Marco Rubio warned him about Huang's relationship with a suspected Chinese spy, The New York Times reported Tuesday. Huang, who had not initially been invited on the trip, joined the president's delegation during a refueling stop in Alaska.

Howard Lutnick had already been sent a highly classified intelligence memo detailing concerns about Huang's relationship, according to the Nov. 10 Axios report.

On May 12, the day Trump departed for China, IREN announced the pricing of a convertible bond offering that ultimately raised $3 billion when it closed two days later. Cantor was one of seven bookrunners on the deal, alongside JPMorgan Chase and Goldman Sachs.

On the day the offering closed, May 14, Lutnick's Commerce Department quietly cleared roughly 10 Chinese companies, including Alibaba and ByteDance, to buy Nvidia's advanced H200 chips, though some of those approvals predated the intelligence memo.

It is unclear when IREN hired Cantor for the offering or how much the firm earned from the deal. Cantor declined to answer CNBC's questions about its work with IREN.

Cantor reported a substantial position in IREN's stock.

By the end of June, Cantor reported $115 million in IREN shares and $362 million in Nvidia shares. Together, the two companies accounted for more than 9% of the value of Cantor's reported $5.2 billion in securities holdings, excluding options, according to CNBC's analysis of the filing.

IREN was not the only company in Nvidia's orbit that turned to Cantor for financing.

In October 2025, Cantor advised AI infrastructure developer Lancium on a $600 million financing deal to support its data center campus in Abilene, Texas, the first site of Stargate, the massive AI infrastructure project backed by OpenAI, SoftBank and Oracle.

Newmark, a commercial real estate firm controlled by Cantor, also helped finance the Abilene campus, arranging a $7.1 billion construction loan in May 2025.

Ten months later, Nvidia agreed to invest $2 billion for a roughly 20% stake in Lancium, with up to $1 billion more tied to future milestones, The Information reported in August. Lancium, which is also backed by Blackstone, confirmed an investment and a technology partnership on Aug. 24 without disclosing financial terms.

It's unclear whether Cantor continued advising Lancium after the financing deal or played a role in Nvidia's investment. Neither Cantor nor Lancium responded to questions about the investment.

Cantor's reported ties also extend to Nvidia's supply chain.

In May 2025, days after Howard Lutnick announced the transfer of his ownership stake in Cantor to his children via trusts, Semafor reported that bankers at the firm had informally advised executives at Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing Co., which makes Nvidia's most advanced chips, on navigating Washington. Cantor called Semafor's report "baseless and false."

TSMC received billions of dollars in federal subsidies created during the Biden administration and administered by the Commerce Department to fund its U.S. manufacturing expansion.

Democratic senators called for an ethics investigation in December into whether the Lutnick family stood to benefit from Howard Lutnick's push to expand AI data centers.

And on Wednesday, Sens. Warren and Chris Coons, D-Del., demanded that Howard Lutnick, Rubio and White House chief of staff Stephen Miller preserve records on Nvidia's chip exports to China and intelligence warnings about Huang.

The senators questioned whether "prudent defensive counter-intelligence action was taken or national security recommendations were overruled."

"The place where I think the Trump administration has most failed here is in being persuaded by Jensen Huang and others to license the export of some advanced chips," Coons said Friday on CNBC's "Squawk Box," accusing the administration of turning a blind eye to the black market for more advanced chips.

#2767. Steelers vs. Bengals Score, Live Updates, Highlights From Week 10 Game

Steelers vs. Bengals Score, Live Updates, Highlights From Week 10 Game originally appeared on SportsNet Pittsburgh. Add SportsNet Pittsburgh as a Preferred Source by clicking here.

The Pittsburgh Steelers struggled to keep pace with the Cincinnati Bengals on Sunday night, falling 38-18 in a divisional matchup at Paycor Stadium. Pittsburgh's offense couldn't do enough to overcome Cincinnati's scoring production, leaving the Steelers with a disappointing loss in Week 11.

Pittsburgh entered the matchup riding a four-game winning streak, but Cincinnati quickly took control. Joe Burrow completed 35 of 44 passes for 409 yards and four touchdowns, while Ja'Marr Chase dominated the Steelers' secondary with 11 receptions for 231 yards. The Bengals scored on four of their first five possessions and took a commanding 24-3 lead into halftime.

The Steelers struggled to generate offense for much of the night, managing just a field goal in the first half. Aaron Rodgers eventually connected with DK Metcalf for a 22-yard touchdown and a two-point conversion in the third quarter to give Pittsburgh some life. However, Cincinnati answered with another touchdown to extend its lead to 38-11. Rodgers later found Pat Freiermuth for a touchdown, but the comeback effort fell short.

The loss drops Pittsburgh to 6-3 and ends its four-game winning streak. Cincinnati improves to 7-2, takes sole possession of first place in the AFC North and extends its winning streak to five games.

#2766. $180 million Van Gogh tests the art market and collector confidence

A version of this article appeared in CNBC's Inside Wealth newsletter, a weekly guide to the high-net-worth investor and consumer. Sign up to receive future editions straight to your inbox.

The art auctions in New York next week could top $2 billion, as a treasure trove of masterpieces comes to the market for the first time in decades.

Total sales at Sotheby's, Christie's and Phillips are expected to be around $2.4 billion, which would equal last year's total, according to ArtTactic. Sotheby's alone is expected to pull in over $1.1 billion, with a single collection from The Blaquier-Arrieta Collection estimated at $450 million.

The collection includes the two big headliners of the week: Vincent van Gogh's landscape "Châtaigniers en fleurs," (Chestnut Trees in Flower) estimated at $180 million and Paul Cézanne's "Arlequin," estimated at $120 million. Neither has been seen in public for a half a century.

Sotheby's CEO Charles Stewart said demand is strong in the art market, especially for the highest quality blue-chip works, as rising stocks markets and private wealth boost the confidence of collectors.

"There's been enormous wealth creation and a lot of confidence in markets," Stewart said. "It's a global phenomenon. We're seeing recovery and greater interest across Asia and something really exciting happening from the Middle East across to Singapore and Europe and the U.S."

Christie's star of the week is Wassily Kandinsky's "Betonte Ecken," estimated at $60 million to $80 million. Christie's CEO Bonnie Brennan describes the market is "confident but discerning, rewarding truly exceptional works." She said recent gyrations in the stock market and fears of an AI bubble aren't likely to deter serious collectors.

"At the very highest end of our market, collectors tend to take a much longer-term view," Brennan said. "They understand that the opportunity to acquire a masterpiece may come only once in a generation, and that can be far more important than what the stock market has done in any particular week. In uncertain moments, the very best can actually become even more compelling."

The art market has continued to rebound this year after declines in 2023 and 2024, with auction sales in the first half up 70% over last year, to $6.8 billion, according to Bank of America.

Drew Watson, head of art services at Bank of America, said blue-chip art is outperforming, with Impressionist & Modern sales up 133% in the first half.

"Our outlook for the art market this November is cautiously optimistic," Watson said. "Improving liquidity, combined with an unusually strong concentration of fresh-to-market private collections, creates a favorable backdrop for the marquee sales."

The Inside Wealth newsletter by Robert Frank is your weekly guide to high-net-worth investors and the industries that serve them.

#2765. Sunday Night Football: Joe Burrow torching Steelers, Bengals lead 24-3 at halftime

Joe Burrow and the Bengals are putting it on the Steelers on Sunday night.

Cincinnati's quarterback threw for 302 yards and three touchdowns in the first two quarters as the Bengals have a 24-3 lead at halftime.

Burrow completed 26-of-34 passes in the first half. Seven completions went to Ja'Marr Chase for 146 yards, including a 53-yard downfield pass. Tee Higgins — who went back to the locker room just before the half for an injury check — caught five passes for 66 yards.

Cincinnati scored on a 24-yard field goal to cap an opening drive, then used a 13-play, 92-yard drive to make the score 10-0 with Burrow's 3-yard touchdown to Drew Sample. Chase Brown then caught a 2-yard touchdown pass to make the score 17-0.

After Chris Boswell put the Steelers on the board with a 45-yard field goal late in the second period, the Bengals got right down the field and scored on Burrow's 15-yard pass to Dohnte Meyers.

The only thing that stopped Cincinnati's offense in the first half was kicker Evan McPherson, who missed his first kick of the season in sending a 43-yard field goal attempt wide right in the second quarter.

Cincinnati's defense has played just as well as its offense. Pittsburgh was limited to just four first downs and 40 total yards in the first half. Aaron Rodgers started the game 0-of-8 passing, finishing the half 4-of-12 for 25 yards.

Rodgers had never started a game 0-of-8 in his career, according to the NBC broadcast. His first completion was a 5-yard pass to Darnell Washington with 4:31 left in the second quarter.

Pittsburgh will get a shot to turn it around to start the third quarter, as the team will receive the second-half kickoff.

#2764. Is a bottom in for the Nasdaq-100? Charts to watch

(See the video above for Frank's complete chart breakdown.)

Is the Nasdaq-100's recent bounce a sign that the bottom is in? We're going to look at several charts to help answer that question.

First, let's remember how we got here. In September, the NDX broke out from a bullish cup-and-handle pattern, producing a 33,200-upside target. That breakout quickly failed, however, and gave way to a bearish pattern that broke down aggressively two weeks ago.

The decline brought the index relatively close to its 26,350 downside target before the current rebound began.

Zooming out, it's telling to see where the bounce originated. Three important forms of support converged in the 25,000-26,000 area:

This confluence of support helped encourage buying after a roughly 15% decline.

For perspective, we also see what larger drawdowns of 20%, 30%, 40%, and 50% would look like … a lot worse.

The following chart tracks NDX drawdowns from 52-week highs going back to just after the financial crisis. Only a handful of declines exceeded 20%, and three produced relatively quick reversals: late 2018, the Covid lows in 2020 and April 2025.

Historically, buying into declines of approximately 15%–25% has generally paid off, even when additional downside occurred first (yellow highlights).

The chart also highlights previous declines of 10%–16% in green, putting the current pullback into perspective.

The major exception was 2022, when the NDX eventually declined nearly 36% over approximately 10 months ... a much worse outcome, which many investors are concerned could happen again now.

That raises the key question: Are we experiencing another routine correction, or the early stages of a larger bear market?

One important clue will be how far this bounce progresses.

Any rally that fails to reclaim the previous highs could establish a significant lower high. Additional lower highs would provide stronger evidence of a developing, longer downtrend.

We saw this in March 2022, when that first sharp rebound effort failed to reach the previous high before a much more severe decline followed.

The bottom line: The current bounce is encouraging, particularly given where it originated and knowing how well buying prior 10-20% drawdowns has paid off.

From here, it's all about the follow through.  We don't need to see new highs right away, but any rally from here that's met with another wave of strong selling could reignite downside momentum.

Frank Cappelleri is the founder and president of CappThesis, an independent research firm that helps active investors through time-tested chart and statistical analysis. Prior to starting CappThesis, he spent 25 years on Wall Street as an equity sales trader, technical analyst, research sales specialist and desk strategist.
